Geometric Categories and Sheaves on Topoi
Connor Bass

Abstract
We introduce the notion of a geometric ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -category, the protopyical example of which is an ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -topos. We study (hyper)sheaves on geometric ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -categories, proving that these are characterized by a form of Čech (hyper)descent. As an application we study (hyper)sheaves on ( n , 1 ) (n,1) -topoi for all n ∈ 𝐙 ≥ 1 ∪ { ∞ } n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\} , and prove that the effective epimorphism topology on an ( n , 1 ) (n,1) -topos 𝒳 \mathcal{X} may be identified as the canonical topology on 𝒳 \mathcal{X} . Moreover, we show that for finite n ∈ 𝐙 ≥ 1 n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1} the study of sheaves on an ( n , 1 ) (n,1) -topos 𝒳 \mathcal{X} is equivalent to the study of ( n − 1 ) (n-1) -truncated sheaves on certain ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -topoi. We then globalize our study to consider sheaves on ∞ ​ 𝒯 ​ o ​ p \infty\mathcal{T}op . In the appendix, we study the behavior of modules under a reflective monoidal ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -functor L ⊗ : 𝒞 ⊗ → 𝒟 ⊗ L^{\otimes}:\mathcal{C}^{\otimes}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\otimes} , and study (hyper)sheafification under a change of universe.



1
Introduction

1.1
Motivation and Approach

In Higher Topos Theory (
[
7
]
) 6.3.5, Lurie introduces the
(\infty,1)
-category
\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X})
of sheaves (of possibly large spaces) on an
(\infty,1)
-topos
\mathcal{X}
, defined as the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
spanned by those maps
\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
which preserve small limits. This is a very well-behaved notion of descent on
\mathcal{X}
, leading to many well-developed higher geometric theories (foundational references for this include
[
6
]
and
[
9
]
). Given the central importance of the
(\infty,1)
-topos (in a larger universe)
\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X})
to higher geometry, it is desirable to better understand the geometry of
\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X})
itself. In particular, consider the following folkloric result (which we prove in
4.1
):

Proposition 1.1.1
.

(Compare to
4.1.1
.) Let
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}
, let
\mathcal{Y}
be an
(n,1)
-topos, and let
\mathcal{D}
be an
(n,1)
-category. Then, a map
F:\mathcal{Y}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a sheaf for the effective epimorphism topology on
\mathcal{Y}
if and only if
F
preserves small limits.

This suggests a meaningful relationship between
\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X})
and sheaves
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})}(\mathcal{X})
for the effective epimorophism topology
\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})
on
\mathcal{X}
. The naïve guess of an identification
\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X})=\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})}(\mathcal{X})
is decidedly
false
(see
3.3.13
for an explicit family of counterexamples), the discrepancy being that in the
n=\infty
case, the map
L_{\mathcal{X}}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X})
left adjoint to the inclusion need not be a topological localization (even in our larger universe). Instead, we are appealing to the following more subtle notion of descent in higher category theory:

Proposition 1.1.2 (HTT 6.3.5.28, HTT 6.5.2.19)
.

There exists a unique Grothendieck topology
J
on
\mathcal{X}
such that
L_{\mathcal{X}}
factors as
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})\xrightarrow{T}\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{C}\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X})
, where
T
is the
J
-sheafification functor, and
C
is a cotopological localization (in our larger universe).

Our first main result is that comparing
\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X})
to
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})}(\mathcal{X})
is not all that unreasonable, in that the topology
J
on
\mathcal{X}
identified in
1.1.2
may always be identified as
\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})
. We prove this as a part of the following theorem, which identifies several of the basic geometric structures of
\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X})
:

Theorem 1.1.3
.

(Compare to
4.2.1
.) Let
\mathcal{X}
be an
(\infty,1)
-topos, and write
\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})
for the effective epimorphism topology on
\mathcal{X}
. Then:

(1)

The inclusion
\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X})\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
factors through
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})}(\mathcal{X})\subset\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
. Moreover, the left adjoint
C:\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X})
induced by HTT 6.3.5.28 is a cotopological localization (in our larger universe).

(2)

For all
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, pulling-back along the localization
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{X}
yields a categorical equivalence
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\tau_{\leq n}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{\sim}\tau_{\leq n-1}\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})}(\mathcal{X})
, where
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\tau_{\leq n}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{X})
is the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Fun}(\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}},\tau_{\leq n}\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
spanned by those maps
F:\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\tau_{\leq n}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
which preserve small limits.

(3)

Pulling-back along
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{hyp}}
yields a categorical equivalence
\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{hyp}})\xrightarrow{\sim}\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})}(\mathcal{X})^{\textnormal{hyp}}
.

We note that in light of
1.1.3

(1)
, the respective classifications of the truncated and hypercomplete objects of
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})}(\mathcal{X})
given by
1.1.3

(2)
and
1.1.3

(3)
also classify the analogous data for
\textnormal{Shv}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{X})
. Theorem
1.1.3
has several immediate consequences, including:

Corollary 1.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a hypercomplete
(\infty,1)
-topos, and let
\mathcal{D}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category. Then, a map
F:\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a hypersheaf for the effective epimorphism topology on
\mathcal{D}
precisely when
F
preserves small limits.

Corollary 1.1.5
.

(Compare to
4.2.6
.) Let
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
, and let
\mathcal{X}
be an
(n,1)
-topos. Then, there exists a unique finest Grothendieck topology
J
on
\mathcal{X}
satisfying the property that every representable functor
\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is a
J
-sheaf. Moreover, this is the effective epimorphism topology on
\mathcal{X}
.

Corollary
1.1.4
may be seen as a higher analogue of
1.1.2
, and
1.1.5
is a verification of the folkloric claim that the effective epimorphism topology on
\mathcal{X}
is in fact the canonical topology on
\mathcal{X}
. Unlike in the classical case for
(1,1)
-topoi, claim
(1)
of
1.1.3
is not inherently obvious. Instead, our proof of
1.1.3
(1) is carried out by first introducing and developing a more general theory of
geometric
(\infty,1)
-categories
, which will occupy section
3.1
:

Definition 1.1.6
.

Fix a regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category with
\kappa
-small coproducts and pullbacks. We call
\mathcal{C}
a
\kappa
-geometric

(\infty,1)
-category if any of the following equivalent conditions hold:

(1)

Said
\kappa
-small coproducts in
\mathcal{C}
are disjoint and universal.

(2)

Changing universe if necessary so that
\mathcal{C}
is small, the reflective localization functor
L_{\kappa}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}^{\times,\kappa}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is left exact, where
\textnormal{Fun}^{\times,\kappa}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
denotes the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
spanned by maps
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
which preserve
\kappa
-small products.

(3)

There exists a subcanonical Grothendieck topology
J
on
\mathcal{C}
such that every
J
-sheaf
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
preserves
\kappa
-small products.

When
\kappa=\Omega
is the strongly inaccessible cardinal bounding our implicitly chosen universe, we will refer an
\Omega
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathcal{C}
as
geometric
, and when
\kappa=\aleph_{0}
is the smallest infinite cardinal, we will refer to an
\aleph_{0}
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathcal{C}
as
finitary
.

We prove the equivalence of conditions
(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
of
1.1.6
(see
3.1.11
and
3.1.13
). There are many important examples of geometric
(\infty,1)
-categories:

Example 1.1.7
.

The following are several examples of
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-categories:

(1)

Any
(n,1)
-topos
\mathcal{X}
is geometric, for all
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
.

(2)

Both
\textnormal{CAlg}^{\textnormal{opp}}
and
\textnormal{CAlg}^{\textnormal{cn},\textnormal{opp}}
are finitary
(\infty,1)
-categories.

(3)

The
(\infty,1)
-category of
(\infty,1)
-topoi,
\infty\mathcal{T}op
, is geometric.

(4)

The
(\infty,1)
-category of spectral Deligne Mumford stacks
SpDM
is geometric.

(5)

The
(1,1)
-category of schemes and the
(1,1)
-category of topological spaces are both geometric.

Each of the
(\infty,1)
-categories in
1.1.7
behaves
geometrically
in some inexact sense, and
1.1.6
is meant to make this sentiment precise. To this point, each satisfies the hypotheses of the following, which may be generalized in the case that
\mathcal{C}
is an
(n,1)
-topos (see
3.2.3
):

Proposition 1.1.8
.

(Compare to
3.2.2
.) Fix a (possibly large) regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category with final object
\bm{1}_{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathcal{C}
. Let
G
be a
\kappa
-small discrete group, and write
G_{\mathcal{C}}
for the group object in
\mathcal{C}
given by the canonical group object structure on
\coprod_{g\in G}\bm{1}_{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathcal{C}
. Then, there exists a categorical equivalence
\Phi:\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{LMon}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{C})
satisfying the property that the composition
\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\Phi}\textnormal{LMon}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{C}
is naturally isomorphic to
\textnormal{ev}_{*}:\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, where
F:\textnormal{LMon}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is the canonical forgetful functor and
*\in\textnormal{B}G
is the lone vertex.

Our main result for geometric
(\infty,1)
-categories is the following generalization of Lurie’s (hyper)sheaf classification result for finitary
(\infty,1)
-categories given by
[
9
]
A.3.3.1 and
[
9
]
A.5.7.2:

Theorem 1.1.9
.

(Compare to
3.3.1
.) Fix a (possibly large) regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category. Let
\tau
be a Grothendieck pretopology on
\mathcal{C}
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

For all
\kappa
-small collections of objects
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}
, the family
\{U_{i}\rightarrow\coprod_{j\in I}U_{j}\}_{i\in I}
is a
\tau
-covering.

(ii)

All
\tau
-coverings
\{V_{k}\rightarrow X\}_{k\in K}
satisfy the property that
K
is
\kappa
-small.

Then, for all
(\infty,1)
-categories
\mathcal{D}
, a map
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a
\tau
-sheaf precisely when
F
satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

F
preserves
\kappa
-small products.

(2)

For all
\tau
-coverings
\{V_{k}\rightarrow X\}_{k\in K}
, the composition
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{U}\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{D}
is a limit diagram, where
U
is the (opposite of the) Čech nerve of
\coprod_{k\in K}V_{k}\rightarrow X
.

Moreover,
F
is a
\tau
-hypersheaf precisely when
F
satisfies (1) along with the following condition:

(2^{\prime})

For all
\tau
-hypercoverings
X_{\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
(as defined in
3.3.5
), the composition
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{X_{\bullet}^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{D}
is a limit diagram.

After invoking
1.1.9
to prove
1.1.3
, we dedicate
4.3
to proving the following global analogue of
1.1.3
:

Proposition 1.1.10
.

(Compare to
4.3.5
.) Let
F:\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
be a sheaf (see
4.3.2
), and write
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})
for the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Fun}((\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
spanned by the sheaves
\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
. Writing
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}_{/F}}(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})
for the full subcategory spanned by the sheaves for the étale topology on
\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F}
(in the sense of
2.2.1
(1)), we then have the following:

(1)

The inclusion
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
factors as the composition
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})\hookrightarrow\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}_{/F}}(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
.

(2)

The inclusion
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})\hookrightarrow\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}_{/F}}(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})
induced by
(1)
admits a cotopologoical (in our larger universe) left adjoint
C:\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}_{/F}}(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})\rightarrow\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})
.

Moreover, the inclusion
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
identifies
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})^{\textnormal{hyp}}
with the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Fun}((\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
spanned by those maps
G:(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
which satisfy the following two properties:

(\textnormal{i})

G
preserves small products.

(\textnormal{ii})

For all augmented semisimplicial objects
X_{\bullet}:(\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F}
satisfying the property that for each
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
the
n
th covering map
X_{n}\rightarrow M_{n}(X)
is both étale and an effective epimorphism of
(\infty,1)
-topoi, the composition
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{X^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\bullet}}(\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{G}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
is a limit diagram.

As a concluding remark of this introduction, a significant portion of the theory of this paper relies on the following folkloric result, which we prove in
2.2
:

Proposition 1.1.11
.

(Compare to
2.2.1
.) Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
(\infty,1)
-category equipped with a topology
J
. Let
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be a map, and write
p:\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
for the right fibration
\mathcal{C}_{/F}:=\mathcal{C}\times_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
classifying
F
. Write
p^{*}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
for the map given by pulling-back along
p^{\textnormal{opp}}:(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}
, and write
p_{!}:\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
and
p_{*}:\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
for the left and right adjoints of
p^{*}
, respectively. Then:

(1)

Let
J_{/F}
be the collection of sieves on objects
U\in\mathcal{C}_{/F}
of the form
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/p(U)}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/p(U)}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/U}
, where
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/p(U)}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{/p(U)}
is a
J
-covering sieve on
p(U)
. Then,
J_{/F}
is a Grothendieck topology on
\mathcal{C}_{/F}
.

(2)

Suppose that
F
is a
J
-sheaf. Then, the categorical equivalence
\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
over
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
given by the left Kan extension of the inclusion
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
along
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
restricts to a categorical equivalence
\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})_{/F}
.

(3)

The adjunction
p^{*}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightleftarrows\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}):p_{*}
restricts to an adjunction
p^{*}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\rightleftarrows\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F}):p_{*}
. Moreover, when
F
is
J
-sheaf, the adjunction
p_{!}:\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightleftarrows\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}):p^{*}
restricts to an adjunction
p_{!}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})\rightleftarrows\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C}):p^{*}
.

(4)

Whenever
F
is a
J
-sheaf, the map
p_{*}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
is an étale morphism of
(\infty,1)
-topoi.

In the appendix (
5
), we further prove the following pair of results, which may be of independent interest:

Proposition 1.1.12
.

(Compare to
5.1.1
.) Let
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes}
be a monoidal
(\infty,1)
-category satisfying the property that
\mathcal{C}
admits geometric realizations and the tensor product
\otimes:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
preserves geometric realizations separately in each variable. Let
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
be a reflective subcategory such that the localization functor
L:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is compatible with the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{C}
, and let
A\in\textnormal{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
. Then:

(1)

The map
L^{\prime}:\textnormal{LMod}_{A}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\textnormal{LMod}_{L(A)}(\mathcal{D})
induced by post-composition with
L^{\otimes}
is a reflective localization.

(2)

An object
M\in\textnormal{LMod}_{A}(\mathcal{C})
is
L^{\prime}
-local if and only if the underlying
\mathcal{C}
-object of
M
is contained in the full subcategory
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
.

Proposition 1.1.13
.

(Compare to
5.2.1
.) Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
(\infty,1)
-category equipped with a Grothendieck topology
J
. Write
i:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
and
i^{\prime}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\hookrightarrow\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
for the corresponding inclusions, let
L:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
denote the sheafification functor, which we identify as an arrow
L:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
, and write
L^{\textnormal{hyp}}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{hyp}}
for the map left adjoint to the inclusion, which we identify as an arrow
L^{\textnormal{hyp}}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
. For each
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}
let
\tau_{\leq n}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\tau_{\leq n}\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
be the corresponding truncation functor, which we identify as an arrow
\tau_{\leq n}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
. Similarly define the maps
L^{\prime}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})\rightarrow\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
,
L^{\prime,\textnormal{hyp}}:\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{hyp}}\subseteq\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
and
\tau^{\prime}_{\leq n}:\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\tau_{\leq n}\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
. Then:

(1)

i^{\prime}\circ L\cong L^{\prime}\circ i
as maps
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
.

(2)

i^{\prime}\circ\tau_{\leq n}\cong\tau^{\prime}_{\leq n}\circ i^{\prime}
as maps
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
.

(3)

i^{\prime}\circ L^{\textnormal{hyp}}\cong L^{\prime,\textnormal{hyp}}\circ i^{\prime}
as maps
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
.

Remark 1.1.14
.

We note that
1.1.13

(1)
already appears in the literature as
[
6
]
2.4.10.

Remark 1.1.15
.

We would also like to acknowledge that Thorger Geiß has independently obtained results similar to
1.1.9
and
1.1.11
. We were made aware of this in a private communication after the completion of these portions of the theoretical component of this work.

Remark 1.1.16
.

As a final note in this introduction, throughout this paper we often assume the existence of a largely unnecessary amount of pullbacks. This is merely for convenience, and many of our results hold in greater generality.

I would like to thank my advisor Mike Hill for his wonderful support and guidance. In addition, I would like to thank Thorger Geiß for suggesting an approach to proving part of
3.3.14
, and I would like to thank Jiacheng Liang for suggesting an approach to proving
5.2.8
. I would like to further thank Thorger Geiß for helpful comments on a draft of this article. This work was partially supported by the Department of Education under Award P200A240046.

1.2
Notation

We will use the Joyal-Lurie model structure on simplicial sets, and we assume a sufficient supply of Grothendieck universes. In the majority of cases we adopt the notation of
[
10
]
or
[
9
]
, defaulting to the notation of
[
10
]
in the case of conflict. However, not all notation used is conventional. A (very incomplete) list of commonly used notation is as follows:

\bullet

\mathcal{S}
will denote the
(\infty,1)
-category of (small) spaces, and
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
will denote the
(\infty,1)
-category of spaces which are possible large.

\bullet

For all pairs of
(\infty,1)
-categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
,
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
will denote the
(\infty,1)
-category of
(\infty,1)
-functors
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
.

\bullet

We will stray from the notation of Kerodon, in that we will not distinguish between a category
\mathcal{C}
and its nerve
\textnormal{N}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})
.

\bullet

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an
(\infty,1)
-topos. Then,
\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{hyp}}
will denote the full subcategory of
\mathcal{X}
spanned by the hypercomplete objects. In addition
\mathcal{X}^{\heartsuit}
(as well as
\tau_{\leq 0}\mathcal{X}
) will be used to denote the full subcategory of
\mathcal{X}
spanned by the discrete objects.

\bullet

\Delta
will denote the (skeletal) simplex category with objects
[n]
for all
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
. The initial object of
\Delta^{\triangleleft}
will be denoted by
[-1]
.
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}
will denote subcategory of
\Delta
spanned by those maps
[m]\rightarrow[n]
which are injective maps of linearly ordered sets, and
\Delta^{\triangleleft}_{\textnormal{inj}}\subset\Delta^{\triangleleft}
is defined similarly.

\bullet

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small
(\infty,1)
-category. Then,
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
will be used to denote the Yoneda embedding. For each
X\in\mathcal{C}
, the representable functor
\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
will be denoted by either
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(-,X)
,
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
, or
h_{X}
, and the map
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
corepresented by
X
will be denoted by either
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,-)
,
h^{X}_{\mathcal{C}}
, or
h^{X}
.

\bullet

Let
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
, and let
\mathcal{X}
be an
(n,1)
-topos. We will write
\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})
for the effective epimorphism topology on
\mathcal{X}
, which we will also refer to as the
canonical topology
on
\mathcal{X}
(see
4.2.6
for a justification of this terminology).

2
Preliminaries on Sheaves

2.1
Topoi, Sheaves, and Sites

We begin with our most essential definitions.

Definition 2.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a presentable
(\infty,1)
-category, and let
S
be a (possibly large) collection of arrows in
\mathcal{C}
. Write
\overline{S}
for the strongly saturated class of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
generated by
S
(
[
7
]
5.5.4.7), and suppose that
\overline{S}
is of small generation (
[
7
]
5.5.4.7). Writing
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
for the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
spanned by the
S
-local objects, one calls a map
L:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
left adjoint to the inclusion
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
(
[
10
]
06VH) a
Bousfield localization of
\mathcal{C}
at
S
.

Remark 2.1.2
.

Under the assumptions of
2.1.1
, the map
L:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
always exists,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is presentable, and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
is accessible (
[
7
]
5.5.4.15).

Definition
2.1.1
is not entirely standard: in
[
7
]
, such
L:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
are simply referred to as
localizations
, which creates a notational conflict with the following:

Definition 2.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category, and let
W
be any collection of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. A
localization of
\mathcal{C}
at
W
is an
(\infty,1)
-category
W^{-1}\mathcal{C}
and an
(\infty,1)
-functor
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow W^{-1}\mathcal{C}
satisfying the property that for all
(\infty,1)
-categories
\mathcal{E}
, the map
F^{*}:\textnormal{Fun}(W^{-1}\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
is fully faithful, and the essential image of
F^{*}
is comprised of those maps
G:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
which carry each
w\in W
to an isomorphism in
\mathcal{E}
.

Under the notation of
2.1.1
, it is not true that
L:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is a localization at
S
. However, it
is
true that such
L
are localizations at
\overline{S}
, and we will (somewhat abusively) use the naked term
localization
to refer to such a Bousfield localization. Moreover, the map
L:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
does still satisfy the following universal property:

Lemma 2.1.4
.

Under the notation of
2.1.1
, let
\mathcal{E}
be a cocomplete
(\infty,1)
-category. Write
\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{E})
for the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{E})
spanned by those maps
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
which preserve small colimits, and define
\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})\subseteq\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
similarly. Then, pulling-back along
L
yields a fully faithful map
\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
, whose essential image is those
(\infty,1)
-functors carrying every edge of
S
to an isomorphism in
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

Combining
[
7
]
5.5.4.15 with
[
10
]
02G3, we deduce that
L
is a localization at
\overline{S}
, in the sense of
2.1.3
. Thus,
L^{*}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
is fully faithful, and the essential image of
L^{*}
is the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
spanned by those arrows
G:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
satisfying the property that, for all
s\in\overline{S}
,
G(s)
is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{E}
. Since
L
preserves small colimits, it is immediately apparent that
L^{*}
restricts to an arrow
\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
. It remains to show that a cococontinuous map
G:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
carries each
s\in\overline{S}
to an isomorphism precisely when
G
carries each
s^{\prime}\in S
to an isomorphism, which follows from
[
7
]
5.5.4.10.

\square

Remark 2.1.5
.

In the case that
\mathcal{E}
is also locally small,
2.1.4
coincides with
[
7
]
5.5.4.20. Moreover, under the notation of
[
9
]
1.3.1.7, the proof of
[
9
]
1.3.1.7 only holds in the case that
\mathcal{C}
is locally small (since this relies on
[
7
]
5.5.4.20). The more general case may be deduced from
2.1.4
.

Definition 2.1.6 (HTT 6.1.0.6, HTT 6.1.5.3, HTT 6.5.2.19)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category. We call
\mathcal{X}
an
(\infty,1)
-topos
if
\mathcal{X}
satisfies any of the following equivalent definitions:

(1)

\mathcal{X}
satisfies the following:

(a)

\mathcal{X}
is presentable.

(b)

(Small) colimits in
\mathcal{X}
are universal.

(c)

Coproducts in
\mathcal{X}
are disjoint.

(d)

For all groupoid objects
U:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
, the colimit diagram
\overline{U}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is a Čech nerve.

(2)

\mathcal{X}
is presentable, and for some regular cardinal
\kappa
such that
\mathcal{X}
is
\kappa
-accessible and
\mathcal{X}_{\leq\kappa}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
is closed under finite limits (which always exists by
[
7
]
5.4.7.4), the map left adjoint to
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{X}_{\leq\kappa})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is left exact.

(3)

There exists a small
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathcal{C}
, a Grothendieck topology
J
on
\mathcal{C}
, and a cotopological localization
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
.

There are many further equivalent variations of
2.1.6
, many of which are covered in detail in
[
7
]
Chapter 6. For the case that
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}
, we will recall the definition of an
(n,1)
-topos for
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}
in
4.1
(see
4.1.3
).

Definition 2.1.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category (which is not necessarily small), and let
J
be a Grothendieck topology on
\mathcal{C}
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be an arbitrary
(\infty,1)
-category. We will say that a map
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a
J
-sheaf
if for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
and
J
-covering sieves
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{/X}
, the composition
(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X})^{\triangleright}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{/X})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{F^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{opp}}
is a colimit diagram.

In practice, many Grothendieck topologies are generated by
sites
:

Definition 2.1.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category with pullbacks. Let
\tau
be a (possibly large) collection of (possibly large) families of morphisms with fixed target
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}
, satisfying the following:

(1)

For all isomorphisms
f:U\rightarrow V
in
\mathcal{C}
,
\{f:U\rightarrow V\}\in\tau
.

(2)

For all
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}\in\tau
and maps
g:U\rightarrow X
, we have that
\{U\times_{X}U_{i}\rightarrow U\}_{i\in I}
is in
\tau
, where the fibre product
U\times_{X}U_{i}
is formed in
\mathcal{C}
.

(3)

If
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}\in\tau
and for each
i\in I
there exists some
\{g_{j_{i}}:V_{j_{i}}\rightarrow U_{i}\}_{j_{i}\in J_{i}}\in\tau
, then
\coprod_{i\in I}\{f_{i}\circ g_{j_{i}}:V_{j_{i}}\rightarrow U_{i}\}_{j_{i}\in J_{i}}\in\tau
for all
\coprod_{i\in I}J_{i}
-tuples of choices of compositions
f_{i}\circ g_{j_{i}}
.

We further make the technical assumption that for all strongly inaccessible cardinals
\Omega
for which
\mathcal{C}
is
\Omega
-essentially small, each
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
is an
\Omega
-small set. Then, we call the pair
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
a
site
, and refer to
\tau
as a (Grothendieck)
pretopology
on
\mathcal{C}
. We will refer to each
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}\in\tau
as a
\tau
-covering
.

Definition 2.1.9
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
be an site. Let
J_{\tau}
be the collection of sieves on objects
X\in\mathcal{C}
, where
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{/X}
is a
J_{\tau}
-sieve on
X
precisely when there exists some
\tau
-covering
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
such that each
f_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}
. Then, we call
J_{\tau}
the
Grothendieck topology on
\mathcal{C}
generated by
\tau
. Noting that
J_{\tau}
is a Grothendieck topology on
\mathcal{C}
, we will refer to a
J_{\tau}
-sheaf
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
as a (
\mathcal{D}
-valued)
\tau
-sheaf
.

Notation 2.1.10
.

In the situation of
2.1.9
, we define
\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C}):=\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{\tau}}(\mathcal{C})
.

When working with a small site
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
, we may use the following to reformulate
2.1.7
:

Lemma 2.1.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
(\infty,1)
-category with pullbacks, and let
\tau
be a Grothendieck pretopology on
\mathcal{C}
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a locally small
(\infty,1)
-category which is also cocomplete, and let
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
be a map. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1)

F
is a
\tau
-sheaf.

(2)

For all
X\in\mathcal{C}
and
\tau
-coverings
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
of
X
, the composition
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{/X}
, the composition
(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X})^{\triangleright}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{/X})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{F^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{opp}}
is a colimit diagram, where
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{/X}
is the sieve on
X
generated by the
f_{i}
.

(3)

For all
\tau
-coverings
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
the composition
\Delta^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{U}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F_{*}}\mathcal{D}
is a limit diagram, where
U
is the (opposite of the) Čech nerve of
\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
, and
F_{*}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is the right Kan extension of
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
along
\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove our claim in the case that
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{S}
. Let
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
be a
\tau
-covering, and let
U:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
be the Čech nerve of
\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
, which we identify as an arrow
U_{0}:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}}
. Extend
U_{0}
to a colimit diagram
\overline{U_{0}}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}}
, noting that
\overline{U_{0}}
carries the
[0]\rightarrow[-1]
edge of
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}
to the map
\Delta^{2}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
giving the epi-mono factorization of
\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
(see
[
10
]
04XJ). Write said epi-mono factorization as
\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}\xrightarrow{e}S\xrightarrow{m}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
. Let
F\in\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
, and identify
\overline{U_{0}}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}}
as a map
\overline{U_{0}}^{\prime}:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}\star\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
. Since that the composition

(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{\overline{U_{0}}}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{(h_{F})^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{S}^{\textnormal{opp}}

is a colimit diagram, it follows that
F
is local with respect to
m:S\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
precisely when the composition

(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{U_{0}}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{(h_{F})^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{S}^{\textnormal{opp}}

is a colimit diagram. Thus, (2) and (3) are equivalent.

We now prove that (1) and (2) are equivalent. Clearly (1) implies (2), so it remains to show the converse. Suppose that (2) holds. Arguing as in the proof of
[
9
]
A.3.3.1, let
X\in\mathcal{C}
, and let
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{/X}
be
\tau
-sieve on
X
. Choose a
\tau
-covering
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
such that each
f_{i}
is an element of
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{/X}
, and write
\mathcal{C}^{1}_{/X}
for the
\tau
-sieve on
X
generated by the
f_{i}
. Noting that the composition
(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X})^{\triangleright}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{/X})^{\triangleright}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{/X})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{F^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{S}^{\textnormal{opp}}
is a colimit diagram, in order to prove that the composition
(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{/X})^{\triangleright}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{/X})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{F^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{S}^{\textnormal{opp}}
is also a colimit diagram, it suffices to show that the composition
\mathcal{C}^{1}_{/X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/X}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{F^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{S}^{\textnormal{opp}}
is left Kan extended from
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}
. Moreover, suffices to prove this latter condition in the case that
\mathcal{C}^{1}_{/X}=\mathcal{C}_{/X}
. Let
g:U\rightarrow X
be a map in
\mathcal{C}
, and consider the map
G:(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{/g})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\textnormal{opp}}
given by the composition

(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{/g})^{\triangleright}\hookrightarrow((\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{/g})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/X}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{F^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{S}^{\textnormal{opp}}.

Let
s:\mathcal{C}_{/U}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{/g}
be a section of the trivial Kan fibration
(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{/g}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/U}
, and pull-back
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{/g}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{/g}
to the sieve

\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}\mathcal{C}_{/U}=\mathcal{C}_{/U}\times_{(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{/g}}(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{/g})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/U}

on
U
generated by the
\tau
-covering
\{U\times_{X}U_{i}\rightarrow U\}_{i\in I}
. Since
F
satisfies condition (2), it follows from
4.1.8
that the composition

(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}\mathcal{C}_{/U})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{/U})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{F^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{S}^{\textnormal{opp}}

is a colimit diagram. Thus, since the composition
(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{/g})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}\mathcal{C}_{/U})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{/U})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{F^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{S}^{\textnormal{opp}}
is equal as a map of simplicial sets to
G
and
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{/g}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}\mathcal{C}_{/U}
is right cofinal (as it is an equivalence), it follows that
G
is also a colimit diagram. This proves our claim.

\square

Remark 2.1.12
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
be an site. Then,
2.1.11
allows for our sheaf condition on
\mathcal{C}
to be reformulated in a familiar fashion. Let
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
be a map. Using the left cofinality of
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}\hookrightarrow\Delta
,
2.1.11
shows that
F
is a
\tau
-sheaf precisely when, for all
\tau
-coverings
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
, the diagram
\Delta^{\triangleleft}_{\textnormal{inj}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
given informally by

F(X)\rightarrow\prod_{i\in I}F(U_{i})\rightrightarrows\prod_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}F(U_{i_{0}}\times_{X}U_{i_{1}})\ \begin{subarray}{c}\rightarrow\\[-9.19998pt]
\rightarrow\\[-9.19998pt]
\rightarrow\end{subarray}\ \cdots

is a limit diagram. Using the fact that the fully faithful embedding
h^{\mathcal{D}}_{\bullet}:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{D}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
preserves small limits in
\mathcal{D}
, a similar statement holds in the case that
\mathcal{D}
is only assumed to admit
I
-indexed products for all
\tau
-coverings
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
(instead of assuming that
\mathcal{D}
is cocomplete). Moreover, using the fact that we have assumed the existence of a sufficient supply of Grothendieck universes, this latter statement may be further generalized to the case that there are no size restrictions on
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
.

2.2
Slices of Topoi

Throughout this paper, we will have an interest in working with sheaves in the relative setting. Our main tool when working in this context will be the following proposition:

Proposition 2.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
(\infty,1)
-category equipped with a topology
J
. Let
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be a map, and write
p:\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
for the right fibration
\mathcal{C}_{/F}:=\mathcal{C}\times_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
classifying
F
. Write
p^{*}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
for the map given by pulling-back along
p^{\textnormal{opp}}:(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}
, and write
p_{!}:\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
and
p_{*}:\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
for the left and right adjoints of
p^{*}
, respectively. Then:

(1)

Let
J_{/F}
be the collection of sieves on objects
U\in\mathcal{C}_{/F}
of the form
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/p(U)}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/p(U)}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/U}
, where
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/p(U)}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{/p(U)}
is a
J
-covering sieve on
p(U)
. Then,
J_{/F}
is a Grothendieck topology on
\mathcal{C}_{/F}
.

(2)

Suppose that
F
is a
J
-sheaf. Then, the categorical equivalence
\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
over
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
given by the left Kan extension of the inclusion
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
along
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
restricts to a categorical equivalence
\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})_{/F}
.

(3)

The adjunction
p^{*}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightleftarrows\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}):p_{*}
restricts to an adjunction
p^{*}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})\rightleftarrows\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F}):p_{*}
. Moreover, when
F
is
J
-sheaf, the adjunction
p_{!}:\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightleftarrows\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}):p^{*}
restricts to an adjunction
p_{!}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})\rightleftarrows\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C}):p^{*}
.

(4)

Whenever
F
is a
J
-sheaf, the map
p_{*}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
is an étale morphism of
(\infty,1)
-topoi.

Proposition 2.2.2
.

Under the notation of
2.2.1
, suppose that
F
is a
J
-sheaf. Let
S
be the isomorphism class of a small set of
\infty
-connective morphisms in
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
, and write
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\wedge}_{S}
for the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
spanned by the
S
-local objects. Let
\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\wedge}_{S_{/F}}
denote the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})
spanned by the
(p_{!})^{-1}(S)
-local objects. Then:

(1)

A morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
is
\infty
-connective in
\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})
if and only if
p_{!}f:p_{!}X\rightarrow p_{!}Y
is
\infty
-connective in
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
. In particular, the inclusion
\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\wedge}_{S_{/F}}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})
admits a cotopological left adjoint.

(2)

The adjunction
p^{*}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightleftarrows\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}):p_{*}
restricts to an adjunction
p^{*}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\wedge}_{S}\rightleftarrows\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\wedge}_{S_{/F}}:p_{*}
. If
F
is
S
-local, the adjunction
p_{!}:\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightleftarrows\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}):p^{*}
restricts to an adjunction
p_{!}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\wedge}_{S_{/F}}\rightleftarrows\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\wedge}_{S}:p^{*}
.

(3)

Whenever
F
is
S
-local, the map
p_{*}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\wedge}_{S_{/F}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\wedge}_{S}
is an étale morphism of
(\infty,1)
-topoi.

Remark 2.2.3
.

Under the notation of
2.2.1
, let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
(\infty,1)
-category equipped with a Grothendieck topology
J
, and let
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be a map. Let
G:(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be an arrow. Then,
p_{!}G:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
may be identified as colimit of the composition
(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/G}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/F}\xrightarrow{p}\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
. Since the composite
(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/G}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a right fibration with essentially small fibres, it follows that the colimit of said composition may be identified as the contravariant transport of the right fibration
(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/G}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
(
[
10
]
03W3). In the case that
F
is a
J
-sheaf, a consequence of
2.2.1
is that
G
is a
J_{/F}
-sheaf if and only if said contravariant transport
\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
of the right fibration
(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/G}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a
J
-sheaf (in fact, this statement is equivalent to
2.2.1

(2)
). This is a generalization of a classical result for fibrations in groupoids of
(1,1)
-categories (combine
[
2
]
06NX with
[
2
]
09WX).

Remark 2.2.4
.

Again under the notation of the notation of
2.2.1
, let
G:(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be a map. Then,
p_{*}G:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
may be identified as the contravariant transport for the right fibration
\mathcal{C}\times_{\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/G}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, where the map
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is given by the composition
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{p^{*}}\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
. This follows from the fact that the composition
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{(p^{*})^{\textnormal{opp}}}\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{h_{G}}\mathcal{S}
is representable by
p_{*}G
.

Remark 2.2.5
.

Under the notation of
2.2.2
, the categorical equivalence
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})_{S}^{\wedge}\xrightarrow{\sim}\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}_{/\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})_{S}^{\wedge}}
of
[
7
]
6.3.5.10 satisfies the following properties:

(1)

Each
F\in\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})_{S}^{\wedge}
is identified with the étale morphism of
(\infty,1)
-topoi
p_{*}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\wedge}_{S_{/F}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\wedge}_{S}
(up to isomorphism).

(2)

Also up to isomorphism, each map
\eta:G\rightarrow H
of
S
-local
J
-sheaves is identified with the map
\eta_{*}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/G}}(\mathcal{C}_{/G})^{\wedge}_{S_{/G}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/H}}(\mathcal{C}_{/H})^{\wedge}_{S_{/H}}
over
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\wedge}_{S}
right adjoint to the map
\eta^{*}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/H}}(\mathcal{C}_{/H})^{\wedge}_{S_{/H}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/G}}(\mathcal{C}_{/G})^{\wedge}_{S_{/G}}
, where
\eta^{*}
is given by pulling-back along the opposite of the map of right fibrations
\eta\circ:\mathcal{C}_{/G}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/H}
over
\mathcal{C}
induced by
\eta
.

(3)

The map
\eta_{*}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/G}}(\mathcal{C}_{/G})^{\wedge}_{S_{/G}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/H}}(\mathcal{C}_{/H})^{\wedge}_{S_{/H}}
in (2) may be identified as the restriction of the map
\eta_{*}:\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/G})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/H})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
right adjoint to the arrow
\eta^{*}:\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/H})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/G})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
, where
\eta^{*}
is given by pulling-back along the opposite of the map of right fibrations
\eta\circ:\mathcal{C}_{/G}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/H}
over
\mathcal{C}
induced by
\eta
.

Remark 2.2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
(\infty,1)
-category equipped with a Grothendieck topology
J
. Let
\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\textnormal{CAlg}
be a
J
-sheaf of
{\mathbb{E}_{\infty}}
-rings on
\mathcal{C}
, which we identify as a sheaf of
{\mathbb{E}_{\infty}}
-rings
\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{X}}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\textnormal{CAlg}
on the
(\infty,1)
-topos
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
. Then,
2.2.1
implies that for each
J
-sheaf
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
, the étale slice of the spectrally ringed
(\infty,1)
-topos
(\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C}),\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{X}})
over
F
may be identified as the pair
(\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F}),\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{X}}|_{F})
, where
\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{X}}|_{F}:\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\textnormal{CAlg}
is the sheaf of
{\mathbb{E}_{\infty}}
-rings on
\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})
corresponding to the
J_{/F}
-sheaf given by the composition
(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}\textnormal{CAlg}
.

Remark 2.2.7
.

Under the notation of
2.2.1
, Proposition
2.2.8
implies that the equivalence
\theta:\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})_{/F}\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})
over
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
which is homotopy inverse to the equivalence
\textnormal{Shv}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})_{/F}
carries an arrow
\eta:E\rightarrow F
to the composition
(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\hookrightarrow(\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}(-,\eta)}\mathcal{S}
. In particular, under the informal identification of the objects of
\mathcal{C}_{/F}
as a pair
(X\in\mathcal{C},x\in F(X))
, the map
\theta(\eta):(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
carries each such pair
(X\in\mathcal{C},x\in F(X))
to the homotopy fibre of
\eta_{X}:E(X)\rightarrow F(X)
over
x\in F(X)
.

Proposition 2.2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
(\infty,1)
-category, let
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be a presheaf on
\mathcal{C}
, and consider the right fibration
\mathcal{C}_{/F}:=\mathcal{C}\times_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Let
L:\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
be the canonical equivalence over
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
specified in
2.2.1
(2), and let
R:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
be the map given by the composition

\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\xrightarrow{h_{\bullet}}\textnormal{Fun}((\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}).

Then,
R
is homotopy inverse to
L
.

Proof.

We first wish to prove that
R
preserves small colimits. Let
X\in\mathcal{C}
, and let
\eta:h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}\rightarrow F
be a map. Identifying the pair
(X,\eta)
as an object of
\mathcal{C}_{/F}
, we observe that the composition

\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{L}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\xrightarrow{h^{\eta}}\mathcal{S}

may be identified as
\textnormal{ev}_{(X,\eta)}
. Since
L
is an equivalence, it follows that
h^{\eta}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
preserves small colimits, implying that
R:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
preserves small colimits. This implies that the composition
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\xrightarrow{R}\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{L}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
is the left Kan extension of the composition
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\xrightarrow{R}\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{L}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
along
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
. Similarly, the composition
\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{L}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{R}\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is the left Kan extension of the composition
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{L}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{R}\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
along
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
. Thus, it suffices to prove the following two claims:

(1)

The composition
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\xrightarrow{R}\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{L}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
.

(2)

The composition
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{L}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\xrightarrow{R}\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is naturally isomorphic to the Yoneda embedding
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
.

Claim
(2)
follows from combining the fact that the composition
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{L}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
, with the fact that
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
is fully faithful (
[
10
]
03LX). Claim
(1)
immediately follow from the fact that
R
is the left Kan extension of the Yoneda embedding
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
along
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}
(again by
[
10
]
03LX).

\square

As a final note before beginning our proof of
2.2.1
, we demonstrate that not all size conditions asked in
2.2.1
are necessary:

Proposition 2.2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a (possibly large)
(\infty,1)
-category equipped with a Grothendieck topology
J
, let
\kappa
be an uncountable regular cardinal, and let
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
be a
J
-sheaf taking values in
\kappa
-small spaces. Write
\textnormal{Shv}^{J}_{<\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
for the full subcategory of
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
spanned by those
E:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
taking values in
\mathcal{S}_{<\kappa}
, and let
\eta:E\rightarrow X
be a vertex of
\textnormal{Shv}^{J}_{<\kappa}(\mathcal{C})_{/F}\subseteq\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})_{/F}
. Then:

(1)

Up to equivalence, the composition
(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\hookrightarrow(\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}(-,\eta)}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
takes values in
\mathcal{S}_{<\kappa}
.

(2)

Using (1) to identify the composition
\textnormal{Shv}^{J}_{<\kappa}(\mathcal{C})_{/F}\hookrightarrow\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J}(\mathcal{C})_{/F}\xrightarrow{\sim}\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{J_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})
as a map
\theta:\textnormal{Shv}^{J}_{<\kappa}(\mathcal{C})_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}^{J_{/F}}_{<\kappa}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})
, we have that
\theta
is an equivalence.

Proof.

[
10
]
05DV implies that given a map of spaces
f:A\rightarrow B
with
B
essentially
\kappa
-small, then
A
is essentially
\kappa
-small if and only if
A
is essentially
\kappa
-small. Our result now follows from
2.2.7
.

\square

Our proof of
2.2.1
will require some preliminaries.

Lemma 2.2.10
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{D}
be an equivalence of
(\infty,1)
-categories. Then:

(1)

The construction
\mathcal{D}^{0}\mapsto\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}^{0}
gives a bijection between replete full subcategories of
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, an explicit inverse is given by the assignment
\mathcal{C}^{0}\mapsto\mathcal{D}\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}^{0}
, where
\mathcal{D}\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}^{0}
is given by pulling-back
\mathcal{C}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
along a map
F^{-1}:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
homotopy inverse to
F
.

(2)

The bijection of
(1)
restricts to a bijection between sieves on
\mathcal{D}
and sieves on
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

We first prove (1). Let
\mathcal{D}^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
be a replete full subcategory (i.e., a full subcategory such that
\mathcal{D}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}
is an isofibration), and note that
\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
is a replete full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
. Write
F^{-1}:\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{D}
for a homotopy inverse to
F
, and consider now the replete full subcategory
\mathcal{D}\times_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}^{0})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}
. We may identify
\mathcal{D}\times_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}^{0})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}
as
\mathcal{D}\times_{F^{-1}\circ F,\mathcal{D},i}\mathcal{D}^{0}
, where
i:\mathcal{D}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}
is the inclusion, noting the projection map
\mathcal{D}\times_{F^{-1}\circ F,\mathcal{D},i}\mathcal{D}^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a replete full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}
. We will proceed to prove that
\mathcal{D}\times_{F^{-1}\circ F,\mathcal{D},i}\mathcal{D}^{0}=\mathcal{D}^{0}
as subsets of
\mathcal{D}
, which will immediately prove our claim. Let
X\in\mathcal{D}\times_{F^{-1}\circ F,\mathcal{D},i}\mathcal{D}^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
, which implies that
F^{-1}(F(X))\in\mathcal{D}^{0}
. Since
F^{-1}(F(X))\cong X
and
\mathcal{D}^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
is replete, it follows that
X\in\mathcal{D}^{0}
. Thus,
\mathcal{D}\times_{F^{-1}\circ F,\mathcal{D},i}\mathcal{D}^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{0}
. Now, let
Y\in\mathcal{D}^{0}
, and note that
F^{-1}(F(Y))\cong Y
, implying that
F^{-1}(F(Y))\in\mathcal{D}^{0}
. Thus,
Y\in\mathcal{D}\times_{F^{-1}\circ F,\mathcal{D},i}\mathcal{D}^{0}
, so
\mathcal{D}\times_{F^{-1}\circ F,\mathcal{D},i}\mathcal{D}^{0}=\mathcal{D}^{0}
.

We now prove (2). Let
\mathcal{D}^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
again be a replete full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}
. If
\mathcal{D}^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
is a sieve on
\mathcal{D}
, then
\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a right fibration, implying that
\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a sieve on
\mathcal{C}
. If
\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a sieve on
\mathcal{C}
, then
\mathcal{D}\times_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}^{0})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a sieve on
\mathcal{D}
. Since
\mathcal{D}^{0}=\mathcal{D}\times_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}^{0})
, this implies that
\mathcal{D}^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
is a sieve on
\mathcal{D}
if and only if
\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a sieve on
\mathcal{C}
, as desired.

\square

Lemma 2.2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a cocomplete
(\infty,1)
-category with pullbacks, satisfying the property that all small colimits in
\mathcal{X}
are universal. Let
\mathcal{X}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
be a small full subcategory satisfying the property that every object in
\mathcal{X}
may be written as a small colimit of objects in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. Let
\overline{S}
be a collection of arrows in
\mathcal{X}
satisfying the property that
\overline{S}
is closed under small colimits in
\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{X})
, and satisfying the property that
\overline{S}
is closed under pullbacks in
\mathcal{X}
. Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a map in
\mathcal{X}
. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1)

The map
f
is contained in
\overline{S}
.

(2)

For all
V\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
and maps
g:V\rightarrow Y
, the pullback
V\times_{Y}X\rightarrow V
is in
\overline{S}
.

Proof.

Since
\overline{S}
is closed under pullbacks, we have that (1) implies (2). We now prove the converse. Choose some small diagram
F:K\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{0}
such that there exists a natural transformation
\eta_{Y}:F\rightarrow\underline{Y}_{K}
in
\textnormal{Fun}(K,\mathcal{X})
exhibiting
Y
as the colimit of
F
. Let
U:\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(K,\mathcal{X})
be a diagram witnessing the pullback of
\underline{f}_{K}:\underline{X}_{K}\rightarrow\underline{Y}_{K}
along
\eta
, which we identify as a map
U^{\prime}:\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1}\times K,\mathcal{X})
from some
\eta_{X}:F^{\prime}\rightarrow\underline{X}_{K}
to
\eta_{Y}:F\rightarrow\underline{Y}_{K}
. Since
\eta_{Y}:F\rightarrow\underline{Y}_{K}
exhibits
Y
as the colimit of
F
, and colimits in
\mathcal{X}
are universal, it follows that
\eta_{X}:F^{\prime}\rightarrow\underline{X}_{K}
exhibits
\underline{X}_{K}
as the colimit of a diagram
F^{\prime}:K\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
. It follows that we may identify
U^{\prime}
as an edge of
\textnormal{Fun}(K,\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{X}))
witnessing
f:X\rightarrow Y
as the colimit of a diagram
\eta^{\prime}:K\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{X})
. It follows from (2) that
\eta^{\prime}
takes values in the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{X})
spanned by
\overline{S}
, so (1) now follows from the fact that
\overline{S}
is closed under small colimits.

\square

Example 2.2.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
(\infty,1)
-category, and let
L:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
be a left exact reflective localization, which is not necessarily accessible. Then,
2.2.11
implies that an edge
\eta:F\rightarrow G
in
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is an
L
-equivalence if and only if, for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
and maps
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}\rightarrow G
, the pullback
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}\times_{G}F\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
is an
L
-equivalence.

Lemma 2.2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category, let
S
be a collection of arrows in
\mathcal{C}
, and let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
be the full subcategory spanned by the
S
-local objects. Let
X\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, and write
p:\mathcal{C}_{/X}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
for the projection. Then,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{/X}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{/X}
may be identified as the full subcategory spanned by the
p^{-1}(S)
-local objects.

Proof.

Let
h:U\rightarrow X
be an object in
\mathcal{C}_{/X}
, which we will denote by
\overline{U}
. Let
f:A\rightarrow B
be a map in
S
. Consider the homotopy commuting square

\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(B,U)
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,U)
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(B,X)
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,X)
f^{*}
h\circ
f^{*}
h\circ

This is a homotopy pullback square if and only if, for each
g:B\rightarrow U
in
\mathcal{C}
, the induced map on homotopy fibres
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(B,U)_{g}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,U)_{g\circ f}
is a homotopy equivalence. Since the bottom arrow is a homotopy equivalence (as
f\in S
and
X\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
), this implies that
f^{*}:\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(B,U)\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,U)
is a homotopy equivalence if and only if each
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(B,U)_{g}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,U)_{g\circ f}
is a homotopy equivalence. Placing
B
over
X
via
h\circ g:B\rightarrow X
(call this
\overline{B}\in\mathcal{C}_{/X}
), and placing
A
over
X
via
h\circ g\circ f:A\rightarrow X
(call this
\overline{A}\in\mathcal{C}_{/X}
), we see that the induced map
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(B,U)_{g}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,U)_{g\circ f}
may be identified as the arrow
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}(\overline{B},\overline{U})\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}(\overline{A},\overline{U})
given by pulling-back along the obvious arrow
\overline{f}:\overline{A}\rightarrow\overline{B}
satisfying
p(\overline{f})=f
. Thus,
\overline{U}\in\mathcal{C}_{/X}
is local with respect to
p^{-1}(S)
precisely when
U\in\mathcal{C}
is local with respect to
S
.

\square

We now prove
2.2.1
:

Proof.

(
2.2.1
.) We first prove
(1)
. Let
V\in\mathcal{C}_{/F}
, noting that
(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/V}\subseteq(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/V}
is a
J_{/F}
-sieve on
V
. Choose some
J
-sieve
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/p(V)}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{/p(V)}
on
p(V)
, and write
(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{0}_{/V}:=\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/p(V)}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/p(V)}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/V}
. Choose some
f:U\rightarrow V
in
\mathcal{C}_{/F}
. The elements of the sieve
f^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{0}_{/V}\subseteq(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/U}
on
U\in\mathcal{C}_{/F}
may be identified as those maps
g:A\rightarrow U
in
\mathcal{C}_{/F}
satisfying the condition that the composition
f\circ g:A\rightarrow V
(which is well-defined up to homotopy) is an element of
(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{0}_{/V}
. Thus for each such
g:A\rightarrow U
in
f^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{0}_{/V}
, the composition
p(f)\circ p(g):p(A)\rightarrow p(V)
may be identified as an object of
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/p(V)}
, implying that
g:A\rightarrow U
is in
p(f)^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/p(V)})\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/p(U)}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/U}
. Next, let
h:B\rightarrow U
be an arrow in
p(f)^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/p(V)})\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/p(U)}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/U}
, noting that
p(f)\circ p(h):p(B)\rightarrow p(V)
is an element of
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/p(V)}
. This implies that
f\circ h:B\rightarrow V
is an element of
(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{0}_{/V}
, so
h:B\rightarrow U
is an element of
f^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{0}_{/V}
. In particular, we have an isomorphism of simplicial sets
f^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{0}_{/V}=p(f)^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/p(V)})\times_{\mathcal{C}_{/p(U)}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/U}
. It follows that
f^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{0}_{/V}\subseteq(\mathcal{C}_{/F})_{/U}
is a
J_{/F}
-covering sieve.

Now let

such that, for all

is a

-sieve on

. Choose a section

on

as sieves on

We may use the proof of

:

Proof.

Remark 2.2.14
.

The proof of
2.2.1

(1)
does not depend at all on the size conditions of the right fibration
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, and as such an analogous claim holds for an arbitrary right fibration
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, with
\mathcal{D}
an
(\infty,1)
-category which is not necessarily small.

3
Geometric Categories

3.1
Geometric Categories and Geometric Sites

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let
\kappa
be a regular cardinal. We call an
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathcal{C}

\kappa
-geometric
if:

(1)

\mathcal{C}
has pullbacks,

(2)

\mathcal{C}
admits
\kappa
-small coproducts, and

(3)

Said
\kappa
-small coproducts in
\mathcal{C}
are disjoint and universal.

In the case that
\kappa=\aleph_{0}
, we will call an
\aleph_{0}
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-catgeory
\mathcal{C}
a
finitary
(\infty,1)
-category
. In the case that
\kappa=\Omega
is the bounding cardinal of our implicitly chosen universe, we will call
\mathcal{C}
a
geometric
(\infty,1)
-category
.

Remark 3.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a finitary
(\infty,1)
-category. Then,
\mathcal{C}
admits finite limits if and only if
\mathcal{C}
is
disjunctive
in the sense of
[
3
]
4.2.

Remark 3.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category, and let
\kappa
and
\kappa^{\prime}
be regular cardinals satisfying
\kappa\leq\kappa^{\prime}
. If
\mathcal{C}
is
\kappa^{\prime}
-geometric, then
\mathcal{C}
is
\kappa
-geometric.

Example 3.1.4
.

The following are several examples of
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-categories:

(1)

Any
(n,1)
-topos
\mathcal{X}
is geometric, for all
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
.

(2)

Both
\textnormal{CAlg}^{\textnormal{opp}}
and
\textnormal{CAlg}^{\textnormal{cn},\textnormal{opp}}
are finitary
(\infty,1)
-categories.

(3)

\infty\mathcal{T}op
is geometric.

(4)

The
(\infty,1)
-categories
SpDM
,
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
,
SpSch
, and
\textnormal{SpSch}^{\textnormal{nc}}
are all geometric.

(5)

If
\mathcal{C}
is a
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category, then for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
we have that
\mathcal{C}_{/X}
is
\kappa
-geometric.

(6)

The (ordinary) category of schemes and the (ordinary) category of topological spaces are both geometric.

(7)

The
(\infty,1)
-category of small
(\infty,1)
-categories is geometric.

We quickly verify
(3)
and (part of)
(4)
of
3.1.4
:

Proposition 3.1.5
.

Small coproducts in
\infty\mathcal{T}op
are disjoint, and colimits of small diagrams in
\infty\mathcal{T}op
whose transition maps are étale, are universal.

Proof.

Combining
[
7
]
6.5.3.8 with
[
7
]
6.3.5.13, we deduce that
\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}
admits small colimits and pullbacks, which are preserved by
\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}\hookrightarrow\infty\mathcal{T}op
. Since for each
(\infty,1)
-topos
\mathcal{X}
we have an equivalence
\mathcal{X}\cong\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}_{/\mathcal{X}}
, the fact that coproducts in
\infty\mathcal{T}op
are disjoint now follows from the fact that for every
(\infty,1)
-topos
\mathcal{X}
, coproducts in
\mathcal{X}
are universal: for a pair of
(\infty,1)
-topoi
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, the diagram
\Lambda^{2}_{2}\rightarrow\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}
exhibiting
\mathcal{X}\coprod\mathcal{Y}
as the coproduct of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
lifts to a colimit diagram
\Lambda^{2}_{2}\rightarrow\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}_{/\mathcal{X}\coprod\mathcal{Y}}
exhibiting the final object as a coproduct of
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\coprod\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\coprod\mathcal{Y}
. Extending
\Lambda^{2}_{2}\rightarrow\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}_{/\mathcal{X}\coprod\mathcal{Y}}
to a pullback square
U:\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}_{/\mathcal{X}\coprod\mathcal{Y}}
, we see that
U(0,0)
is initial in
\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}_{/\mathcal{X}\coprod\mathcal{Y}}
, implying that the composition
\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}\xrightarrow{U}\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}_{/\mathcal{X}\coprod\mathcal{Y}}\rightarrow\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}
carries
(0,0)\in\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}
to an initial object in
\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}
. This part of our claim now follows from the fact that said composition is a pullback square.

Next, given a geometric morphism of
(\infty,1)
-topoi
f_{*}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, the pullback functor
\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/\mathcal{Y}}\rightarrow\infty\mathcal{T}op_{/\mathcal{X}}
restricts to a map
\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}_{/\mathcal{Y}}\rightarrow\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}_{/\mathcal{X}}
, which upon applying the equivalences
\mathcal{X}\cong\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}_{/\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathcal{Y}\cong\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}_{/\mathcal{Y}}
, we may identify as the map
f^{*}:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
left adjoint to
f_{*}
(combine the proof of
[
7
]
6.3.5.21 with
[
9
]
14.4.2.4). This proves the remainder of our claim.

\square

Corollary 3.1.6
.

Small coproducts in
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
are disjoint, and colimits of small diagrams in
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
whose transition maps are étale, are universal.

Proof.

Applying
[
9
]
21.4.6.4, we have that
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc},\textnormal{\'{e}t}}
admits small colimits, which are preserved by
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc},\textnormal{\'{e}t}}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
. Moreover, since the class of étale morphisms in
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
is stable under pullbacks, the cartesian fibration
\textnormal{ev}_{1}:\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}})\rightarrow\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
restricts to a cartesian fibration
\textnormal{Fun}^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}})\rightarrow\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
. Since for each
\mathsf{X}=(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{X}})\in\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
the canonical map
(\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc},\textnormal{\'{e}t}})_{/\mathsf{X}}\rightarrow\infty\mathcal{T}op^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}_{/\mathcal{X}}
is an equivalence (
[
9
]
21.4.6.4 (5)) and the map
F:\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}})\rightarrow\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}\times_{\infty\mathcal{T}op}\textnormal{Fun}^{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(\Delta^{1},\infty\mathcal{T}op)
induced by the forgetful functor
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}\rightarrow\infty\mathcal{T}op
preserves cartesian arrows (
[
9
]
21.4.6.7), we see that
F
is an equivalence of cartesian fibrations over
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
(
[
10
]
023M). It now follows from
3.1.5
that colimits of small diagrams in
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
whose transition maps are étale, are universal. It remains to show that (small) coproducts in
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
are disjoint. Since for each pair
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}\in\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
the arrows
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}\rightarrow\mathsf{X}\coprod\mathsf{Y}
are étale and
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}\rightarrow\infty\mathcal{T}op
preserves pullbacks along étale morphisms (
[
9
]
21.4.6.7), the fact that (small) coproducts in
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
are disjoint now follows from combining
3.1.5
with the fact that the initial
(\infty,1)
-topos admits an essentially unique structure of a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack (which is then necessarily an initial object of
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
).

\square

Such
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-categories may all be endowed the following structure of an
(\infty,1)
-site:

Definition 3.1.7
.

Fix a regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category. We will refer to the topology on
\mathcal{C}
consisting of those
\kappa
-small coverings of the form
\{U_{i}\rightarrow\coprod_{j\in I}U_{j}\}_{i\in I}
as the
\kappa
