-topology
on
\mathcal{C}
. For any
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathcal{D}
and map
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, we will call
F
a (
\mathcal{D}
-valued)
\kappa
-sheaf
if
F
satisfies descent with respect to the
\kappa
-topology. We will write
\textnormal{Shv}^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{C})
for the
(\infty,1)
-category of
\mathcal{D}
-valued
\kappa
-sheaves, and when
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{S}
we will write
\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
for the
(\infty,1)
-category of
\kappa
-sheaves on
\mathcal{C}
. In the case that
\kappa=\aleph_{0}
, we will refer to the
\aleph_{0}
-topology on
\mathcal{C}
as the
finitary topology
or the
fin
-topology
, and we will call an
\aleph_{0}
-sheaf
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
a
finitary sheaf
or a
fin
-sheaf
.

The key result of this section is the following:

Proposition 3.1.8
.

Fix a regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category. Then, for all
(\infty,1)
-categories
\mathcal{D}
, a map
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a sheaf for the
\kappa
-topology on
\mathcal{C}
if and only if
F
preserves
\kappa
-small products.

Proof.

Changing universe if necessary, assume that both
\mathcal{C}
and
\kappa
are small, and that
\mathcal{D}
is locally small. Since
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a sheaf for the
\kappa
-topology on
\mathcal{C}
if and only if the composition
\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{D}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(U,-)}\mathcal{S}
is a sheaf for the
\kappa
-topology on
\mathcal{C}
for all
U\in\mathcal{D}
, and
\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{D}
preserves
\kappa
-small products if and only if each such composition
\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{D}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(U,-)}\mathcal{S}
preserves
\kappa
-small products, it suffices to prove our claim in the simplified case that
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{S}
. Applying
2.1.12
, it suffices to prove that a map
G:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
preserves
\kappa
-small products if and only if, for all
\kappa
-small sets
\{U_{i}\in\mathcal{C}\}_{i\in I}
of objects in
\mathcal{C}
the composition
\Delta^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{\overline{U}}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}(-,G)}\mathcal{S}
is a limit diagram, where
\overline{U}
is the (opposite of the) Čech nerve of
\coprod_{j\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{j}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\coprod_{i\in I}U_{i}}
. To begin, one inductively shows that (the opposite of)
\overline{U}
carries the
[n]\rightarrow[-1]
edge of
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}
to a map of the form
(\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}})\coprod(\coprod_{j\in J}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\varnothing_{\mathcal{C}}})\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\coprod_{i\in I}{U_{i}}}
, where
J
is some (small) set,
\varnothing_{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathcal{C}
is an initial object, and
\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\coprod_{i\in I}{U_{i}}}
is the canonical map. If
G:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
preserves
\kappa
-small products, then
\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}(\overline{U}(-),G)
carries each edge of the form
[-1]\rightarrow[n]
in
\Delta^{\triangleleft}
to an isomorphism in
\mathcal{S}
. By 2-out-of-3, it follows that
\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}(\overline{U}(-),G)
carries every edge of
\Delta^{\triangleleft}
to an isomorphism in
\mathcal{S}
, and the fact that the composition
\Delta^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{\overline{U}}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}(-,G)}\mathcal{S}
is a limit diagram may now be deduced from the fact that
\Delta
is weakly contractible.

To see the converse, suppose now that the map
G:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is a
\kappa
-sheaf, which implies that
\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}(\overline{U}(-),G):\Delta^{\triangleleft}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is a limit diagram (
2.1.12
). Since the empty sieve
\varnothing\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/\varnothing_{\mathcal{C}}}
is a covering sieve for the
\kappa
-topology on
\mathcal{C}
, it follows that
G(\varnothing_{\mathcal{C}})\in\mathcal{S}
is final. Writing
U
for the underlying cosimplicial object of
\overline{U}
, it follows that the map
\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}(U(-),G):\Delta\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
carries every edge of
\Delta
to an isomorphism, which immediately implies that
\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}(\overline{U}(-),G):\Delta^{\triangleleft}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
carries every edge of
\Delta^{\triangleleft}
to an isomorphism, proving our claim.

\square

Remark 3.1.9
.

Write
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}
for the subcategory of
\Delta
spanned by the injective morphisms. It follows from the left cofinality of the inclusion
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}\hookrightarrow\Delta
that the statement analogous to
2.1.11
using
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}
in place of
\Delta
also holds.

In particular, we have the following:

Corollary 3.1.10
.

Let
\kappa
be a small regular cardinal, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
\kappa
-geometic
(\infty,1)
-category. Write
\textnormal{Fun}^{\times,\kappa}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
for the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
spanned by those maps
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
which preserve
\kappa
-small products. Then, the localization
L:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}^{\times,\kappa}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is left exact.

In good cases the converse also holds:

Proposition 3.1.11
.

Let
\kappa
be a small regular cardinal, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
(\infty,1)
-category which admits
\kappa
-small coproducts, and has pullbacks. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1)

The localization
L_{\kappa}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}^{\times,\kappa}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is left exact.

(2)

\mathcal{C}
is a
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category.

Proof.

The fact that
(2)
implies
(1)
is established in
3.1.10
. Assume now that
(1)
holds. We first prove that
\kappa
-small coproducts in
\mathcal{C}
are universal. Let
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be some
\kappa
-small collection of objects in
\mathcal{C}
, and let
f:X\rightarrow\coprod_{i\in I}U_{i}=:U
be a map. We wish to show that, for all
Y\in\mathcal{C}
, the arrow
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)\rightarrow\prod_{j\in I}\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X\times_{U}U_{j},Y)
is a homotopy equivalence. Since
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{Y}:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
preserves
\kappa
-small products, it suffices to show that the arrow
\coprod_{j\in I}(h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X\times_{U}U_{j}})\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
is an
L_{\kappa}
-equivalence. However, since
\coprod_{j\in I}(h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X\times_{U}U_{j}})\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
may be identified as the map
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}\times_{h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U}}\coprod_{j\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{j}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
, this follows from the fact that the class of
L_{\kappa}
-equivalences is stable under pullback (
[
7
]
6.2.1.1).

Next, let
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
. Since
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}\coprod h^{\mathcal{C}}_{Y}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X\coprod Y}
is an
L_{\kappa}
-equivalence and
L_{\kappa}
is left exact, it follows that we may identify
L_{\kappa}(h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}\times_{h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}\coprod h^{\mathcal{C}}_{Y}}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{Y})\cong L(\underline{\varnothing}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}})
as the fibre product
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}\times_{h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X\coprod Y}}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{Y}\cong h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X\times_{X\coprod Y}Y}
in
\textnormal{Fun}^{\times,\kappa}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
. Our claim now follows from the fact that
\underline{\varnothing}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\varnothing_{\mathcal{C}}}
is an
L_{\kappa}
-equivalence, where
\varnothing_{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathcal{C}
is initial.

\square

Remark 3.1.12
.

Proposition
3.1.11
admits several variations. In particular,
(1)
may be replaced with:

(1^{\prime})

The localization
L_{\kappa}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}^{\times,\kappa}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is topological.

Corollary 3.1.13
.

Let
\kappa
be a (possibly large) regular cardinal, and let
\mathcal{C}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category which admits
\kappa
-small coproducts, and has pullbacks. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{C}
is a
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category.

(2)

There exists a subcanonical Grothendieck topology
J
on
\mathcal{C}
such that all
J
-sheaves preserve
\kappa
-small products.

One may also identify the
\kappa
-topology by means of a universal property:

Proposition 3.1.14
.

Fix a small regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category. Then, for all cocomplete
(\infty,1)
-categories
\mathcal{E}
, pulling-back along
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
gives a categorical equivalence

\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}^{\amalg,\kappa}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E}),

where
\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{E})
is the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{E})
spanned by those maps
F:\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
which preserve small colimits, and
\textnormal{Fun}^{\amalg,\kappa}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
is the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
spanned by those maps
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
which preserve
\kappa
-small coproducts.

Proof.

The universal property of
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
yields an equivalence
\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}),\mathcal{E})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
. Moreover, pulling-back along
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
yields a fully faithful
(\infty,1)
-functor
\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}),\mathcal{E})
, the essential of which consists of those maps
F:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
carrying each
\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\coprod_{j\in I}U_{j}}
to an isomorphism, where
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is some
\kappa
-small collections of objects in
\mathcal{C}
(
2.1.4
). Thus, the composition
\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}^{co\ell im}(\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}),\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
is fully faithful, and the essential image of said composition consists precisely of those maps
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
which preserve
\kappa
-small coproducts, as desired.

\square

Corollary 3.1.15
.

Fix a small regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category which admits finite limits. Then, for all
(\infty,1)
-topoi
\mathcal{Y}
, pulling-back along
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
yields a categorical equivalence

\textnormal{Fun}^{*}(\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{Y})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Y})^{\prime},

where
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Y})^{\prime}\subseteq\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Y})
denotes the full subcategory spanned by those maps
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
which preserve
\kappa
-small coproducts, and are left exact.

Proof.

Applying
[
7
]
6.2.3.20, we see that pulling-back along
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
yields a fully faithful
(\infty,1)
-functor
\textnormal{Fun}^{*}(\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Y})
, the essential image of which is spanned by the maps
G:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
which are left exact, and satisfy the property that for all
\kappa
-small collections
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of objects in
\mathcal{C}
, the arrow
\coprod_{i\in I}G(U_{i})\rightarrow G(\coprod_{j\in I}U_{i})
is an effective epimorphism in
\mathcal{Y}
. Since
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Y})^{\prime}\subseteq\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Y})
is contained in the essential image of
\textnormal{Fun}^{*}(\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Y})
, it suffices to prove that for left exact left adjoints
(\infty,1)
-topoi
f^{*}:\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, the composition
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{f^{*}}\mathcal{Y}
preserves
\kappa
-small coproducts. Our claim now follows from
3.1.14
.

\square

The geometry of the
(\infty,1)
-topos
\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
is in part captured by the following proposition:

Proposition 3.1.16
.

Fix a small regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category. Then:

(1)

For each
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}
, the map
\tau_{\leq n}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{S})
restricts to the map
\tau_{\leq n}:\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\tau_{\leq n}\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
.

(1)

An arrow
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
is an effective epimorphism if and only if the image of
f
under the inclusion
\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is an effective epimorphism.

(2)

The
(\infty,1)
-topos
\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
is hypercomplete.

Proof.

Claim
(1)
follows from the fact that
\tau_{\leq n}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{S}
preserves (
\kappa
-)small products. One direction of
(2)
is obvious. To prove the converse, suppose that
\eta:F\rightarrow G
is an effective epimorphism in
\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
. We wish to show that for each
X\in\mathcal{C}
, the map
\eta_{X}:F(X)\rightarrow G(X)
is an effective epimorphism of spaces. It follows from
[
4
]
3.12 that
\tau_{\leq 0}F\rightarrow\tau_{\leq 0}G
is an effective epimorphism in
\tau_{\leq 0}\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
. Moreover,
(1)
implies that
\tau_{\leq 0}\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})\cong\textnormal{Shv}^{\kappa}_{\textnormal{Sets}}(\textnormal{h}\mathcal{C})
, and that the canonical map
\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}^{\kappa}_{\textnormal{Sets}}(\textnormal{h}\mathcal{C})
carries each
A
to the arrow
(\textnormal{h}\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Sets}
induced by the composition
\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{A}\mathcal{S}\xrightarrow{\pi_{0}(\cdot)}\textnormal{Sets}
. In particular, these facts together imply that for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
and
u\in G(X)
there exists some
\kappa
-small set of maps
\{f_{i}:X_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}
such that the arrow
\coprod_{i\in I}X_{i}\rightarrow X
is an equivalence, and for each
i\in I
the image of
u
in
G(X_{i})
is in the image of
\eta_{X_{i}}:F(X_{i})\rightarrow G(X_{i})
. Since
F(X)\rightarrow\prod_{i\in I}F(X_{i})
and
G(X)\rightarrow\prod_{i\in I}G(X_{i})
are equivalences, it immediately follows that
u\in G(X)
is in the image of
F(X)\rightarrow G(X)
, completing the proof of
(2)
. To see
(3)
, we observe that
(2)
implies that for each
H\in\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
a simplicial object
U_{\bullet}:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})_{/H}
is a hypercovering of
\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})_{/H}
(in the sense of
[
7
]
6.5.3.2) if and only if the composition
\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{U_{\bullet}}\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})_{/X}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/H}
is a hypercovering of
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/H}
. In particular, the colimit of said composition is final in
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/H}
, implying that the colimit of
U_{\bullet}
is final in
\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})_{/H}
, so
U_{\bullet}
is effective (in the sense of
[
7
]
6.5.3.2). Invoking
[
7
]
6.5.3.12 now proves our claim.

\square

We now turn our attention to studying an especially well-behaved class of topologies on
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-categories.

Definition 3.1.17
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category, and let
\tau
be a Grothendieck pretopology on
\mathcal{C}
. We call a pair
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
a
\kappa
-geometric site
if all coverings in
\tau
are
\kappa
-small, and if
\tau
is finer than the
\kappa
-pretopology. When
\kappa=\aleph_{0}
we will call an
\aleph_{0}
-site
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
a
finitary site
.

Remark 3.1.18
.

A more detailed study of finitary sites
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
may be found in section A of the appendix of
[
9
]
.

Example 3.1.19
.

The following are all examples of
\kappa
-geometric sites:

(1)

The pair
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X}))
is a geometric site, where
\mathcal{X}
is an
(n,1)
-topos for
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
, and
\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})
is the effective epimorphism pretopology on
\mathcal{X}
. We will sometimes refer to
\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})
as the
canonical topology
on
\mathcal{X}
: see
4.2.6
for a justification of this name.

(2)

The pair
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X}))
is generated by a finitary site, where
\mathcal{X}
is a coherent
(\infty,1)
-topos.

(3)

The pair
(\textnormal{CAlg}^{\textnormal{cn},\textnormal{opp}},\textnormal{fpqc})
is a finitary site. So too are each of the other standard pretopologies on affine spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. Moreover, the analogous statement holds when considering affine nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks.

(4)

The pair
(\infty\mathcal{T}op,\textnormal{\'{e}t})
is a geometric site, where
ét
denotes the étale pretopology on
\infty\mathcal{T}op
.

(5)

The étale topology on
SpDM
,
\textnormal{SpDM}^{\textnormal{nc}}
,
SpSch
, and
\textnormal{SpSch}^{\textnormal{nc}}
are each generated by a geometric site. The analogous statements hold for the Zariski, Nisnevich, and fpqc topologies.

(6)

Let
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
be a
\kappa
-geometric site, where
\mathcal{C}
is a locally small
(\infty,1)
-category. Let
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be a
\kappa
-sheaf on
\mathcal{C}
classified by the right fibration
\mathcal{C}\times_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}=:\mathcal{C}_{/F}\xrightarrow{p}\mathcal{C}
, and write
\tau_{/F}
for the pretopology on
\mathcal{C}_{/F}
where
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}\in\tau_{/F}
precisely when
\{p(f_{i}):p(U_{i})\rightarrow p(X)\}_{i\in I}\in\tau
. Then,
(\mathcal{C}_{/F},\tau_{/F})
is a
\kappa
-geometric site.

The following lemma (which we will make important use of in
4.3
) may be used to verify
3.1.19
(6):

Lemma 3.1.20
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small
(\infty,1)
-category and let
p:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be a right fibration with essentially small fibres classifying a presheaf
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
. Let
K
be a small simplicial set, let
U:K\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be a diagram admitting a colimit
X\in\mathcal{C}
, and let
\overline{U}:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be a diagram exhibiting
X\in\mathcal{C}
as said colimit of
F
. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1)

Every lift of
U
along
p
to an arrow
K\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
admits an extension to diagram
K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
over
\overline{U}
, and every such extension is a colimit diagram.

(2)

Every lift of
U
along
p
to an arrow
\widetilde{U}:K\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
may be extended to a colimit diagram
K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, and an arbitrary extension
Z:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
of
\widetilde{U}
is a colimit diagram if and only if the composition
K^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{Z}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{p}\mathcal{C}
is a colimit diagram.

(3)

The composition
(K^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{\overline{U}^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{S}
is a limit diagram.

Suppose now that
\mathcal{C}
admits
K
-indexed colimits. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1^{\prime})

\mathcal{E}
admits
K
-indexed colimits, and a diagram
\overline{V}:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is a colimit diagram if and only if the composition
K^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{\overline{V}}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{p}\mathcal{C}
is a colimit diagram.

(2^{\prime})

F
preserves
K^{\textnormal{opp}}
-indexed limits.

We will prove
3.1.20
at the end of this section.

Remark 3.1.21
.

Let
\kappa
and
\kappa^{\prime}
be regular cardinals, and assume that
\kappa^{\prime}\leq\kappa
. Then, for any
\kappa
-geometric site
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
, we have that
(\mathcal{C},\tau^{\prime})
is a
\kappa^{\prime}
-geometric site, where
\tau^{\prime}
is the pretopology on
\mathcal{C}
whose coverings are the subset of
\tau
-coverings
\{U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
where
I
is
\kappa^{\prime}
-small.

Under the notation of
3.1.19
, one can use the following to classify sheaves on
\kappa
-geometric sites of the form
(\mathcal{C}_{/F},\tau_{/F})
, whenever
F
is a
\tau
-sheaf:

Lemma 3.1.22
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
(\infty,1)
-category with pullbacks, and let
\tau
be a pretopology on
\mathcal{C}
. Let
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be a
\tau
-sheaf, and write
p:\mathcal{C}_{/F}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
for the right fibration classifying
F
. Write
\tau_{/F}
for the pretopology on
\mathcal{C}_{/F}
whose coverings are those families of maps
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow U\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}_{/F}
satisfying the property that
\{p(f_{i}):p(U_{i})\rightarrow p(U)\}_{i\in I}
is a
\tau
-covering. Then, the equivalence of
(\infty,1)
-categories
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
of
2.2.8
restricts to an equivalence
\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})_{/F}\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau_{/F}}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})
.

Proof.

Combine
2.2.1
with
2.2.8
.

\square

Corollary 3.1.23
.

Let
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
, let
\mathcal{X}
be an
(n,1)
-topos, and let
Z\in\mathcal{X}
. Then, the canonical equivalence
\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{X}_{/Z})^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}},\widehat{\mathcal{S}})_{/h_{Z}}
restricts to an equivalence
\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X}_{/Z})}(\mathcal{X}_{/Z})\xrightarrow{\sim}\widehat{\textnormal{Shv}}_{\mathcal{C}an(\mathcal{X})}(\mathcal{X})_{/h^{\mathcal{X}}_{Z}}
.

Corollary 3.1.24
.

Fix a small regular cardinal
\kappa
, let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category, and let
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be a map which preserves
\kappa
-small products. Then, the canonical categorical equivalence
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Fun}((\mathcal{C}_{/F})^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
restricts to a categorical equivalence
\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})_{/F}\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}_{/F})
.

Proof.

(
3.1.20
.) To begin, we show that
(1)
is equivalent to
(2)
. Supposing that
(1)
holds, choose some lift
\widetilde{U}:K\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
of
U
along
p
(noting if no such
\widetilde{U}
exists, then
(2)
is vacuously true). It follows immediately that
\widetilde{U}
may be extended to a colimit diagram
Z^{\prime}:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
. Next, suppose we are given an arbitrary extension
Z:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
of
\widetilde{U}
. If the composition
K^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{Z}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{p}\mathcal{C}
is
not
a colimit diagram, then
Z
is not isomorphic to
Z^{\prime}
, and as such, is not a colimit diagram. Suppose now that the composition
K^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{Z}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{p}\mathcal{C}
is a colimit diagram, and consider the right fibration
\textnormal{Fun}(K^{\triangleright},\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(K^{\triangleright},\mathcal{C})
. There exists an isomorphism
\eta:p\circ Z\xrightarrow{\sim}\overline{U}
, which yields an isomorphism in
\textnormal{Fun}(K^{\triangleright},\mathcal{E})
from
Z
to some arrow
K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
over
\overline{U}
(since right fibrations are conservative, see
[
10
]
019K). Next, suppose that
(2)
holds, and choose some lift
\widetilde{U}:K\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
of
U
along
p
. In order to prove that
(1)
holds, it suffices to show that there exists an extension of
\widetilde{U}
over
\overline{U}:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. However, extending
\widetilde{U}:K\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
to a colimit diagram
Z:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
, we have an isomorphism of diagram
K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
from
\overline{U}
to
p\circ Z
, so the fact that
\textnormal{Fun}(K^{\triangleright},\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(K^{\triangleright},\mathcal{C})
is a right fibration proves our claim.

We now show that
(1)
implies
(3)
. Suppose that
(1)
holds. Combining
[
10
]
02KR and
[
10
]
02ZA, we deduce that every lift of
\overline{U}
along
p
to an arrow
K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is
p
-left Kan extended from
K\subset K^{\triangleright}
. Consider now the map
\textnormal{Fun}(K^{\triangleright},\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(K,\mathcal{E})\times_{\textnormal{Fun}(K,\mathcal{C})}\textnormal{Fun}(K^{\triangleright},\mathcal{C})
, and pull-back along the inclusion
\textnormal{Fun}_{/\mathcal{C}}(K,\mathcal{E})=\textnormal{Fun}(K,\mathcal{E})\times_{\textnormal{Fun}(K,\mathcal{C})}\{\overline{U}\}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(K,\mathcal{E})\times_{\textnormal{Fun}(K,\mathcal{C})}\textnormal{Fun}(K^{\triangleright},\mathcal{C})
to yield the restriction map
\phi:\textnormal{Fun}_{/\mathcal{C}}(K^{\triangleright},\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}_{/\mathcal{C}}(K,\mathcal{E})
. It now follows from
[
10
]
030R that
\phi
is a trivial Kan fibration, so
(3)
follows from combining
[
7
]
3.3.3.3 with
[
10
]
04CE. Next we wish to show that
(3)
implies
(1)
, so suppose that
(3)
holds. Combining
[
7
]
3.3.3.3 with
[
10
]
04CE implies that
\phi:\textnormal{Fun}_{/\mathcal{C}}(K^{\triangleright},\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}_{/\mathcal{C}}(K,\mathcal{E})
is a trivial Kan fibration. If
\textnormal{Fun}_{/\mathcal{C}}(K,\mathcal{E})
is empty then
(1)
holds vacuously, so suppose that
\textnormal{Fun}_{/\mathcal{C}}(K,\mathcal{E})
is nonempty. Choosing now some map
G:K\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
over
U:K\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, which we extend to a map
\overline{G}:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
over
\overline{U}
(noting such a map
\overline{G}
exists since
\phi
is a trivial Kan fibration), it remains to show that
\overline{G}:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is a colimit diagram. Since it now suffices to prove that any diagram isomorphic to
\overline{G}:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is a colimit diagram, it suffices to prove that every lift of
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ\overline{U}:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}^{\textnormal{rep}}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
along the right fibration
\textnormal{Fun}^{\textnormal{rep}}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}:=\textnormal{Fun}^{\textnormal{rep}}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\times_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}^{\textnormal{rep}}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is a colimit diagram. Let
H\in\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
be the colimit of
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ U
, which yields a canonical map
f:H\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
. Writing
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\prime}
for the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
which are
f
-local, we deduce that the map
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ\overline{U}:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\prime}
is a colimit diagram. Noting also that
F\in\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\prime}
, it follows from
[
10
]
02KC that every lift of
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ\overline{U}:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\prime}
along
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\prime}_{/F}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\prime}
is a colimit diagram. Our claim now follows from the fact that the map
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ\overline{U}:K^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\prime}
takes values in
\textnormal{Fun}^{\textnormal{rep}}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\subseteq\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\prime}
.

The equivalence of
(1^{\prime})
and
(2^{\prime})
now follows from the equivalence of
(2)
and
(3)
, where we allow
U
to vary over
\textnormal{Fun}(K,\mathcal{C})
.

\square

3.2
Application: External vs. Internal Group Actions

Whenever considering an
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathcal{C}
, there are at least two possible interpretations of a “group action on an object
X\in\mathcal{C}
”:

(1)

An
external
action of a discrete group
G
on
X\in\mathcal{C}
via an
(\infty,1)
-functor
\rho:\textnormal{B}G\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
which carries the lone vertex of
\textnormal{B}G
to
X\in\mathcal{C}
.

(2)

An
internal
action of a group object
G:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
on
X\in\mathcal{C}
via some diagram
\rho:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}\times\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
in
\textnormal{LMon}_{G}(\mathcal{C})
, where the underlying
\mathcal{C}
-object of
\rho\in\textnormal{LMon}_{G}(\mathcal{C})
may be identified as
X
.

In many classical geometric settings,
(2)
is a generalization of
(1)
:

Example 3.2.1
.

For a discrete group
G
, we have the following:

(i)

The category of (Borel)
G
-equivariant topological spaces is canonically equivalent to the category of topological spaces equipped with a left
G^{top}
-action, where
G^{top}
denotes the discrete topological group associated to
G
.

(ii)

For a commutative ring
R
, the category of schemes over
R
equipped with a left
G
-action is canonically equivalent to the the category of
R
-schemes equipped with a left
G_{R}
-action, where
G_{R}
denotes the discrete group scheme over
R
associated to
G
.

Informally, (i) identifies a Borel equivariant
G
-space
X
with the left
G^{top}
-space
a:G^{top}\times X\rightarrow X
, where said action map
a:G^{top}\times X\rightarrow X
is identified as the composition
G^{top}\times X\xrightarrow{\sim}\coprod_{g\in G}X\rightarrow X
, where for each
g\in G
, the corresponding map
X\rightarrow X
is induced by the left action of
g\in G
on
X
. The identification given in (ii) is similar.

As an application of
3.1.8
, one can show that the relationships of
3.2.1
carry over to the
(\infty,1)
-categorical setting:

Proposition 3.2.2
.

Fix a (possibly large) regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category with final object
\bm{1}_{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathcal{C}
. Let
G
be a
\kappa
-small discrete group, and write
G_{\mathcal{C}}
for the group object in
\mathcal{C}
given by the canonical group object structure on
\coprod_{g\in G}\bm{1}_{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathcal{C}
. Then, there exists a categorical equivalence
\Phi:\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{LMon}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{C})
satisfying the property that the composition
\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\Phi}\textnormal{LMon}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{C}
is naturally isomorphic to
\textnormal{ev}_{*}:\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, where
F:\textnormal{LMon}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is the canonical forgetful functor and
*\in\textnormal{B}G
is the lone vertex.

Proof.

Just as in the proof of
3.1.8
, we may reduce to the case that
\mathcal{C}
and
\kappa
are both small. Consider the fully faithful
(\infty,1)
-functor
\phi:\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/\underline{\textnormal{B}G}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}
given by the composition
\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{S}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/\underline{\textnormal{B}G}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}
. Since
\underline{\textnormal{B}G}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}
along with its canonical base point
\underline{\Delta}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}\rightarrow\underline{\textnormal{B}G}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}
may be identified as an object of
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\leq 1}_{*}
, one can use
[
8
]
5.2.6.28 to induce an equivalence
\theta:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/\underline{\textnormal{B}G}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{LMon}_{\underline{G}_{{}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}}(\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
. Moreover, said equivalence
\theta:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/\underline{\textnormal{B}G}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{LMon}_{\underline{G}_{{}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}}(\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
is over
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
, where
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/\underline{\textnormal{B}G}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is induced by basechange along
\underline{\Delta^{0}}_{{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}\rightarrow\underline{\textnormal{B}G}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}
(see
[
8
]
5.2.6.29). We next wish to verify that the map
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{S}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/\underline{\textnormal{B}G}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}
, which we will denote by
\gamma
, is over
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
. It suffices to show that the composition
\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{S}_{/\textnormal{B}G}\xrightarrow{\Delta^{0}\times_{\textnormal{B}G}(\cdot)}\mathcal{S}_{/\Delta^{0}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{S}
is corepresented by the lone vertex
*\in\textnormal{B}G
. It follows from
[
7
]
5.5.2.9 that said composition is a right adjoint, implying the existence of a left adjoint
L:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{S})
. Combining the fact that the map left adjoint to the composition
\mathcal{S}_{/\textnormal{B}G}\xrightarrow{\Delta^{0}\times_{\textnormal{B}G}(\cdot)}\mathcal{S}_{/\Delta^{0}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{S}
carries
\Delta^{0}
to an object (isomorphic to)
\Delta^{0}\rightarrow\textnormal{B}G
, along with the observation that the categorical equivalence
\mathcal{S}_{/\textnormal{B}G}\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{LFib}(\textnormal{B}G)
carries
*\rightarrow\textnormal{B}G
to a left fibration
U_{*}\rightarrow\textnormal{B}G
corepresentable by
*\in\textnormal{B}G
(see
[
10
]
043M), we have that
L
carries
\Delta^{0}
to a functor corepresented by
*
, proving this part of our claim. Thus, the composition

\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))\xrightarrow{\gamma}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})_{/\underline{\textnormal{B}G}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}\xrightarrow{\theta}\textnormal{LMon}_{\underline{G}_{{}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}}(\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))

is over
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
. Moreover,
\theta\circ\phi:\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\textnormal{LMon}_{\underline{G}_{{}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}}(\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
is a fully faithful embedding, whose essential image is precisely
\textnormal{LMon}_{\underline{G}_{{}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}}(\textnormal{Fun}^{\textnormal{rep}}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))\subseteq\textnormal{LMon}_{\underline{G}_{{}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}}(\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
.

Next, since the sheafification functor
L_{\kappa}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
is symmetric monoidal with respect to the relevant cartesian symmetric monoidal structures, the map
\Psi:\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\textnormal{LMon}_{L_{\kappa}(\underline{G}_{{}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}})}(\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))
given by the composition

\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\theta\circ\phi}\textnormal{LMon}_{\underline{G}_{{}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}}(\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))\xrightarrow{L_{\kappa}^{\times}}\textnormal{LMon}_{L_{\kappa}(\underline{G}_{{}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}})}(\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))

induces an
(\infty,1)
-functor
\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\textnormal{LMon}_{h^{\mathcal{C}}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}}(\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))
(here we have used the fact that the canonical map of group objects
\underline{G}_{{}_{\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}
is an
L_{\kappa}
-equivalence). Applying
5.1.1
, we deduce that
\Psi
is fully faithful with essential image
\textnormal{LMon}_{h^{\mathcal{C}}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}}(\textnormal{Shv}^{\textnormal{rep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))
, where
\textnormal{Shv}^{\textnormal{rep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
denotes the full subcategory of
\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
spanned by the representable functors. Furthermore, the composition
\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\Phi}\textnormal{LMon}_{h^{\mathcal{C}}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}}(\textnormal{Shv}^{\textnormal{rep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}^{\textnormal{rep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
may be identified as the composition
\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\textnormal{ev}_{*}}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{h_{\bullet}^{\mathcal{C}}}\textnormal{Shv}^{\textnormal{rep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
. Writing
\Theta:\textnormal{LMon}_{h^{\mathcal{C}}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}}(\textnormal{Shv}^{\textnormal{rep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{LMon}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{C})
for the equivalence induced by the map
\textnormal{Shv}^{\textnormal{rep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{C}
inverse to the Yoneda embedding, we may choose
\Phi
to be the arrow
\Theta\circ\Psi:\textnormal{Fun}(\textnormal{B}G,\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\textnormal{LMon}_{G_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{C})
.

\square

Remark 3.2.3
.

In the case that
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{X}
is an
(\infty,1)
-topos, the contents of
3.2.2
can be generalized. In particular, let
H
be a small connected space, and let
L:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
denote the (left exact) left adjoint to the Yoneda embedding. Then, by a similar argument to the one presented in the proof of
3.2.2
, there exists an equivalence
\textnormal{Fun}(H,\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{LMon}_{L(\underline{\Omega H}_{\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}}})}(\mathcal{X})
over
\mathcal{X}
. In the case that
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{Y}
is an
(n,1)
-topos for
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}
, we instead have a canonical equivalence
\textnormal{Fun}(H,\mathcal{Y})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{LMon}_{L^{\prime}(\underline{\Omega(\tau_{\leq n}H)})}(\mathcal{Y})
over
\mathcal{Y}
, where
L^{\prime}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{Y}^{\textnormal{opp}},\tau_{\leq n-1}\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is the (left exact) left adjoint to the inclusion (
4.2.7
).

3.3
Čech (Hyper)descent

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 3.3.1
.

Fix a (possibly large) regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
be a
\kappa
-geometric site. Then, for all
(\infty,1)
-categories
\mathcal{D}
, a map
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a
\tau
-sheaf if and only if
F
satisfies the following two conditions:

(1)

F
preserves
\kappa
-small products.

(2)

For all
\tau
-coverings
\{U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
, the composition
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{U}\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{D}
is a limit diagram, where
U
is the (opposite of the) Čech nerve of the induced map
\coprod_{i\in I}U_{i}\rightarrow X
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Moreover,
F
is a
\tau
-hypersheaf precisely when
F
satisfies (1) along with the following condition:

(2^{\prime})

For all
\tau
-hypercoverings
X_{\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
(as defined in
3.3.5
), the composition
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{X_{\bullet}^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{D}
is a limit diagram.

We first make precise what is meant by the terms
\tau
-hypersheaf
and
\tau
-hypercovering
in
3.3.1
.

Definition 3.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category equipped with a Grothendieck topology
J
, and let
\mathcal{D}
be any other
(\infty,1)
-category. Choose some strongly inaccessible cardinal
\Omega^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{C}
is
\Omega^{\prime}
-small, and
\mathcal{D}
is
\Omega^{\prime}
-locally small. We say that a map
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a
J
-hypersheaf
if for all
D\in\mathcal{D}
, the composition
\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{D}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(D,-)}\mathcal{S}_{<\Omega^{\prime}}
is a hypercomplete object of the
\Omega^{\prime}
-
(\infty,1)
-topos
\textnormal{Shv}^{J}_{<\Omega^{\prime}}(\mathcal{C})
.

Remark 3.3.3
.

Under the notation of
3.3.2
, the property that a map
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a
J
-hypersheaf is independent of choice of
\Omega^{\prime}
(see
5.2.8
).

In order to define a
\tau
-hypercovering
, we will need the following lemma:

Lemma 3.3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category with pullbacks, and choose some
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq-1}
. Write
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}
for the subcategory of
\Delta
spanned by the injective morphisms, and write
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n}
for the full subcategory of
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}
spanned by those
[m]
where
m\leq n
. Let
X_{\leq n,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be a diagram. Then:

(1)

The right Kan extension of
X_{\leq n,\bullet}
along
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}
exists.

(2)

For all
m\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq n}
, the right Kan extension of
X_{\leq n,\bullet}
along
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq m})^{\triangleright}
exists, and is given by the restriction of the map
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
induced by (1).

(3)

Let
X^{R,n}_{\leq n+1,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be the map induced by
(2)
, and define
M_{n+1}(X):=X^{R,n}_{\leq n+1,n+1}\in\mathcal{C}
. Then, there exists a canonical equivalence of
(\infty,1)
-categories

\textnormal{Fun}((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright},\mathcal{C})\times_{\textnormal{Fun}((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright},\mathcal{C})}\{X_{\leq n,\bullet}\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{C}_{/M_{n+1}(X)}.

(4)

Choose an arrow
f:U\rightarrow M_{n+1}(X)
in
\mathcal{C}
, and let
U_{\leq n+1,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be the map induced by (3). Let
\mu:U_{\leq n+1,\bullet}\rightarrow X^{R,n}_{\leq n+1,\bullet}
be the natural transformation induced by (2), which we identify as an edge of
\textnormal{Fun}((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright},\mathcal{C})\times_{\textnormal{Fun}((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright},\mathcal{C})}\{X_{\leq n,\bullet}\}
. Then, the edge
\mu_{n+1}:U_{\leq n+1,n+1}\rightarrow M_{n+1}(X)
in
\mathcal{C}
is isomorphic to the edge
f:U\rightarrow M_{n+1}(X)
.

Definition 3.3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category which admits pullbacks, and choose some augmented semisimplicial object
X_{\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. For each
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, write
\textnormal{cosk}_{n-1}X_{\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
for the right Kan extension of
X_{\bullet}|_{(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n-1})^{\triangleright}}
along
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n-1})^{\triangleright}\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}
, and consider the canonical map
\mu:X_{\bullet}\rightarrow\textnormal{cosk}_{n-1}X_{\bullet}
. We will refer to the edge
\mu_{n}:X_{n}\rightarrow M_{n}(X):=\textnormal{cosk}_{n-1}X_{n}
of
\mathcal{C}
as the
n
th covering map
of
X_{\bullet}
.

Next, choose a regular cardinal
\kappa
, and suppose that
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is a
\kappa
-geometric site. We will call
X_{\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
a
\tau
-hypercovering
, or a
hypercovering for the
\tau
-topology on
\mathcal{C}
if for all
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, the
n
th covering map
\mu_{n}:X_{n}\rightarrow M_{n}(X_{\bullet})
is isomorphic to an arrow of the form
\coprod_{i\in I}U_{i}\rightarrow M_{n}(X)
, where
\{U_{i}\rightarrow M_{n}(X)\}_{i\in I}
is some (
\kappa
-small)
\tau
-covering.

Example 3.3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
(\infty,1)
-category which admits pullbacks, and let
X_{\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be an augmented simplicial object of
\mathcal{C}
. Then, the
0
th covering map
X_{0}\rightarrow M_{0}(X)
may be identified as the arrow
X_{0}\rightarrow X_{-1}
.

Example 3.3.7
.

Fix a regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
be a
\kappa
-geometric site. Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a map in
\mathcal{C}
. Then, the Čech nerve
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
of
f
is a
\tau
-hypercovering if and only if
f:X\rightarrow Y
is isomorphic to an arrow of the form
\coprod_{i\in I}U_{i}\rightarrow Y
, where
\{U_{i}\rightarrow Y\}_{i\in I}
is some (
\kappa
-small)
\tau
-covering.

Example 3.3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an
(\infty,1)
-topos, and let
U_{\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
be a diagram, which we identify as a map
U^{\prime}_{\bullet}:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{/U_{-1}}
. Then,
U_{\bullet}
is a hypercovering for the effective epimorphism topology on
\mathcal{X}
precisely when
U^{\prime}_{\bullet}
is a hypercovering of
\mathcal{X}_{/U_{-1}}
, in the sense of
[
9
]
A.5.2.3.

Proof.

(
3.3.4
.) We first prove
(1)
. Identify
X_{\leq n,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
with a map
X_{\leq n,\bullet}^{\prime}:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/X_{-1}}
, where
X_{-1}
denotes the value of
X_{\bullet}
at the cone point of
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}
. Since
\mathcal{C}
has pullbacks,
\mathcal{C}_{/X_{\leq n,-1}}
has finite limits. In particular, the map
X_{\leq n,\bullet}^{\prime}:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/X_{\leq n,-1}}
admits a right Kan extension to an arrow
X_{\bullet}^{R,n,\prime}:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/X_{\leq n,-1}}
(see the proof of
[
10
]
05H7), which we identify as a map
X_{\bullet}^{R,n,\prime}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Invoking
[
10
]
04K2, we have that
X_{\bullet}^{R,n,\prime}
is right Kan extended from
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}
, proving
(1)
. Claim
(2)
now follows from
[
10
]
0312.

We now prove
(3)
. To begin, observe that the factorization system
(L,R)
endows
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}
with the structure of a Reedy category (in the sense of
[
7
]
A.2.9.1), where
L
is the collection of all morphisms in
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}
, and
R
is the collection of isomorphisms in
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}
. Applying
[
7
]
A.2.9.14, we have that for all
(\infty,1)
-categories
\mathcal{D}
, the canonical map of
(\infty,1)
-categories

\textnormal{Fun}((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright},\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright})_{[n+1]/})^{\triangleleft},\mathcal{D})\times_{\textnormal{Fun}(((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright})_{[n+1]/},\mathcal{D})}\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n},\mathcal{D})

is a categorical equivalence. In particular, for any diagram
F:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, the arrow

\{F\}\times_{\textnormal{Fun}((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright},\mathcal{D})}\textnormal{Fun}((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright},\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}((((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright})_{[n+1]/})^{\triangleleft},\mathcal{D})\times_{\textnormal{Fun}(((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright})_{[n+1]/},\mathcal{D})}\{F^{\prime}\}

is an equivalence, where
F^{\prime}
denotes the composition
((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright})_{[n+1]/}\rightarrow(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{D}
.
[
10
]
04J4 supplies an equivalence
\textnormal{Fun}((((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright})_{[n+1]/})^{\triangleleft},\mathcal{D})\times_{\textnormal{Fun}(((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright})_{[n+1]/},\mathcal{D})}\{F^{\prime}\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{D}_{/F^{\prime}}
, yielding an equivalence
\{F\}\times_{\textnormal{Fun}((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright},\mathcal{D})}\textnormal{Fun}((\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright},\mathcal{D})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{D}_{/F^{\prime}}
. Suppose now that
F
admits a right Kan extension along
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright}
. Then,
\mathcal{D}_{/F^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is representable, so
(3)
immediately follows. Claim
(4)
follows from the proof of
(3)
.

\square

We will prove
3.3.1
in pieces. To begin, we consider when an arrow
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is a
\tau
-sheaf:

Proposition 3.3.9 (Čech Descent)
.

Fix a (possibly large) regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category. Let
\tau
be a Grothendieck pretopology on
\mathcal{C}
such that the pair
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is a
\kappa
-geometric site. Then, for all
(\infty,1)
-categories
\mathcal{D}
, a map
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a
\tau
-sheaf if and only if
F
satisfies the following two conditions:

(1)

F
preserves
\kappa
-small products.

(2)

For all
\tau
-coverings
\{U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
, the composition
\Delta^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{U}\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{D}
is a limit diagram, where
U
is the (opposite of the) Čech nerve of the induced map
\coprod_{i\in I}U_{i}\rightarrow X
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Just as in the proof of
3.1.8
, we may reduce to the case that
\mathcal{C}
is small, and we may suppose that
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{S}
. We begin by proving that all
\tau
-sheaves
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
satisfy
(1)
and
(2)
. Since
\tau
is a finer pretopology than the
\kappa
-(pre)topology, condition
(1)
is automatically satisfied by
F
. We now show
(2)
. Let
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
be a
\tau
-covering, and let
A:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be the Čech nerve of
\coprod_{i\in I}U_{i}\rightarrow X
, and let
B:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
be the Čech nerve of
\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
. Writing
L_{\tau}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})
for the
\tau
-sheafification functor, it follows from
(1)
that the compositions
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{A}\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{L_{\tau}}\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})
and
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{B}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{L_{\tau}}\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})
agree. Since
2.1.11
implies that this latter composition is a colimit diagram, we deduce that the composition
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{A}\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\xrightarrow{L_{\tau}}\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})
is also a colimit diagram, proving
(2)
.

Next, suppose that
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is a map satisfying
(1)
and
(2)
. Applying
2.1.11
, it suffices to show that, for all
\tau
-coverings
\{f_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
, the composition
\Delta^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{B^{\textnormal{opp}}}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}(-,F)}\mathcal{S}
is a limit diagram, where
B
denotes the Čech nerve of
\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
. Applying
(2)
, we deduce that the composition
\Delta^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{A^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}(-,F)}\mathcal{S}
is a limit diagram, where
A
denotes the Čech nerve of
\coprod_{i\in I}U_{i}\rightarrow X
. Thus, it suffices to show that the compositions
\Delta^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{A^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}(-,F)}\mathcal{S}
and
\Delta^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{B^{\textnormal{opp}}}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}(-,F)}\mathcal{S}
coincide. Writing
L_{\kappa}:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
for the sheafification functor, we observe that
(1)
implies that
F\in\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
. Thus,
\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})}(-,F):\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
factors as
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{L_{\kappa}^{\textnormal{opp}}}\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})}(-,F)}\mathcal{S}
. Moreover, since
L_{\kappa}
is left exact, the compositions

\Delta^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{A^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{L_{\kappa}^{\textnormal{opp}}}\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})}(-,F)}\mathcal{S}

and

\Delta^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{B^{\textnormal{opp}}}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{L_{\kappa}^{\textnormal{opp}}}\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Shv}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})}(-,F)}\mathcal{S}

coincide, completing the proof of our claim.

\square

Remark 3.3.10
.

Combining the left cofinality of
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}\hookrightarrow\Delta
with the fact that all Čech nerves
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
may be promoted to a Čech nerve
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
(up to isomorphism),
3.3.9
is equivalent to the sheaf condition stated in
3.3.1
.

Example 3.3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an
(n,1)
-topos for
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
. It follows from
3.3.9
that for any
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathcal{D}
, a map
F:\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a sheaf for the canonical topology on
\mathcal{X}
if and only if the following two conditions hold:

(1)

F
preserves small products.

(2)

Let
G:(\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\textnormal{opp}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
be the Čech nerve of an effective epimorphism. Then, the composition
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{G^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{D}
is a limit diagram in
\mathcal{D}
.

Corollary 3.3.12
.

Under the assumptions of
3.3.9
, suppose also that
\mathcal{C}
is a locally small
(\infty,1)
-category. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1)

For each
\tau
-covering
\{U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
, the induced map
\coprod_{i\in I}U_{i}\rightarrow X
is an effective epimorphism in
\mathcal{C}
.

(2)

The topology on
\mathcal{C}
generated by
\tau
is subcanonical.

Remark 3.3.13
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an
(\infty,1)
-topos. Example
3.3.11
provides a plethora of counterexamples to the often made, yet incorrect claim that “a map
F:\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
preserves small limits if and only if
F
is a sheaf for the effective epimorphism topology on
\mathcal{X}
”. We were first made aware of this family of counterexamples via an online comment of Marc Hoyois: https://chat.stackexchange.com/transcript/message/52499804#52499804. Indeed, let
\mathcal{C}
be a small
(\infty,1)
-category equipped with a Grothendieck topology
J
such that
(1)

\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
is
not
hypercomplete, and
(2)
every representable functor
\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is a hypercomplete
J
-sheaf. It follows from
[
7
]
6.5.2.16 that a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{hyp}}\subset\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
is an effective epimorphism in
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{hyp}}
if and only if
f:X\rightarrow Y
is an effective epimorphism in
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
, implying that the inclusion
i:\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{hyp}}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
preserves effective epimorphisms. Since
i
preserves pullbacks and small coproducts (
[
9
]
D.6.7.3), we deduce that for all
J
-sheaves
H:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
the composition
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{hyp},\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{i^{\textnormal{opp}}}\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{h_{H}}\mathcal{S}
is a sheaf for the effective epimorphism topology on
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
(
3.3.11
). However, since every representable functor
\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is a
J
-hypersheaf, it follows (from a very similar logic as that used in the proof of
4.1.8
) that said composition
\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{hyp},\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{i^{\textnormal{opp}}}\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{h_{H}}\mathcal{S}
preserves small limits if and only if
H
is a
J
-hypersheaf. Choosing some
H\in\textnormal{Shv}_{J}(\mathcal{C})
which is
not
hypercomplete now provides our desired counterexample.

Applying
4.2.6
, we further observe that if there exists a Grothendieck topology
J
on
\mathcal{X}
such that
J
-sheaves
F:\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
are precisely the representable maps, then
J
is the effective epimorphism topology on
\mathcal{X}
. Combining this fact with the above family of counterexamples, we recover
[
9
]
1.3.1.5: there exists
(\infty,1)
-topoi
\mathcal{Y}
such that there
does not
exist a Grothendieck topology
J^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{Y}
satisfying the property that
J^{\prime}
-sheaves
G:\mathcal{X}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
are precisely the representable functors.

We now prove the remainder of
3.3.1
. The main arguments used are either from the proof of
[
9
]
A.5.7.2, or as suggested by Thorger Geiß:

Corollary 3.3.14 (Čech Hyperdescent)
.

Fix a (possibly large) regular cardinal
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\kappa
-geometric
(\infty,1)
-category. Let
\tau
be a Grothendieck pretopology on
\mathcal{C}
such that the pair
(\mathcal{C},\tau)
is a
\kappa
-geometric site. Then, for all
(\infty,1)
-categories
\mathcal{D}
, a map
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a
\tau
-hypersheaf (in the sense of
3.3.2
) precisely when the following two conditions hold:

(1)

F
preserves
\kappa
-small products.

(2)

For all
\tau
-hypercoverings
X_{\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
(as defined in
3.3.5
), the composition
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{X_{\bullet}^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{D}
is a limit diagram.

Proof.

Just as in the proofs of
3.1.8
and
3.3.9
, it suffices to prove our claim in the case that
\mathcal{C}
is small and
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{S}
(see also
3.3.3
). To begin, suppose that
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is a
\tau
-hypersheaf, noting that
3.3.9
implies that that
F
satisfies condition
(1)
. Next, let
X_{\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be a
\tau
-hypercovering, which we identify as an arrow
X_{\bullet}^{\prime}:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/X_{-1}}
. Writing
L:\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})
for the sheafification functor, it follows from the fact that
L
is left exact that the induced map
\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})_{/L(h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X_{-1}})}
is a hypercovering, in the sense of
[
9
]
A.5.2.3 (see
3.3.8
). Since the colimit of
\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})_{/L(h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X_{-1}})}
is
\infty
-connective (see
[
9
]
A.5.3.3), it follows immediately that for all
\tau
-hypersheaves
F
, the composition

\Delta^{\triangleleft}_{\textnormal{inj}}\xrightarrow{X_{\bullet}^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{(h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet})^{\textnormal{opp}}}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{L^{\textnormal{opp}}}\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{h_{F}}\mathcal{S}

is a limit diagram. Since this agrees (up to natural isomorphism) with the composition
\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{X_{\bullet}^{\textnormal{opp}}}\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{S}
, this proves (2).

We now prove the converse. To begin, suppose that
F:\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
satisfies conditions
(1)
and
(2)
. Combining
3.3.7
with
3.3.9
, we deduce that
F
is a
\tau
-sheaf. Since we have shown that all
\tau
-hypersheaves satisfy condition (2), in order to show that
F
is hypercomplete it suffices to prove the following:

(*)

Let
F
and
G
be
\tau
-sheaves on
\mathcal{C}
which satisfy condition (2). Then, any
\infty
-connective map
\eta:F\rightarrow G
in
\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})
is an isomorphism.

In order to prove
(*)
, we will inductively verify that for all such
\eta:F\rightarrow F
and
X\in\mathcal{C}
, the map
\eta_{X}:F(X)\rightarrow G(X)
is
n
-connective in
\mathcal{S}
for all
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
. Temporarily delaying the proof of the base case
n=0
, let
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and suppose that for each
X\in\mathcal{C}
we have that
\eta_{X}:F(X)\rightarrow G(X)
is
n
-connective. In order to show that
\eta_{X}:F(X)\rightarrow G(X)
is
(n+1)
-connective, it suffices to show that each
F(X)\rightarrow F(X)\times_{G(X)}F(X)
is
n
-connective (
[
10
]
04HA (b)). However, noting that the collection of
\tau
-sheaves in
\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})
satisfying
(2)
is closed under all existing limits in
\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})
, the fact that
F(X)\rightarrow F(X)\times_{G(X)}F(X)
is
n
-connective follows from applying our inductive assumption to the arrow
F\rightarrow F\times_{G}F
(which is also an
\infty
-connective map: see
[
7
]
6.5.1.18). So, in order to complete the proof of our claim, it suffices to prove the following:

(*^{\prime})

Let
\eta:F\rightarrow G
be an
\infty
-connective map of
\tau
-sheaves satisfying condition
(2)
. Then, for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
, the map
\eta_{X}:F(X)\rightarrow G(X)
is a
0
-connective map of spaces.

For the remainder of the proof, we fix such a map
\eta:F\rightarrow G
. Let
X\in\mathcal{C}
, and let
Z_{\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be a
\tau
-hypercovering of
X\in\mathcal{C}
. Since
F
and
G
satisfy
(2)
, we have canonical equivalences
F(X)\cong\textnormal{Hom}(h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ Z^{s}_{\bullet},\underline{F})
and
G(X)\cong\textnormal{Hom}(h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ Z^{s}_{\bullet},\underline{G})
(where these are mapping spaces in
\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\textnormal{opp}},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
), where
Z^{s}_{\bullet}
denotes the underlying semisimplicial object of
Z_{\bullet}
. Under these identifications, the map
\eta_{X}:F(X)\rightarrow G(X)
may be identified as the arrow
\textnormal{Hom}(h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ Z^{s}_{\bullet},\underline{F})\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ Z^{s}_{\bullet},\underline{G})
given by post-composition with
\underline{\eta}:\underline{F}\rightarrow\underline{G}
. Thus, in order to prove
(*^{\prime})
, it suffices to show that for all
u:h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}\rightarrow G(X)
there exists a
\tau
-hypercovering
X_{\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
of
X\in\mathcal{C}
and a natural transformation
\nu:h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ X^{s}_{\bullet}\rightarrow\underline{F}
in
\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\textnormal{opp}},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
such that the composition
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ X^{s}_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\nu}\underline{F}\xrightarrow{\underline{\eta}}\underline{G}
coincides with the composition
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ X^{s}_{\bullet}\rightarrow\underline{h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}}\xrightarrow{\underline{u}}\underline{G}
, where
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ X^{s}_{\bullet}\rightarrow\underline{h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}}
is the canonical map. Fixing some
u:h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}\rightarrow G
, we will now apply
3.3.4
to inductively construct a collection of maps
U_{\leq n,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
(as
n
varies over
{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
) satisfying the following properties:

(i)

U_{\leq n,\bullet}
is equal (as a map of simplicial sets) to the restriction of
U_{\leq n+1,\bullet}
to
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}
.

(ii)

U_{\leq n,-1}:\{-1\}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
is the edge
u:h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}\rightarrow G
.

(iii)

Every vertex in the image of the composition
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{U_{\leq n,\bullet}}\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))\xrightarrow{\textnormal{ev}_{0}}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is representable, and when identified as an arrow
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, is a hypercovering of
X\in\mathcal{C}
(up to isomorphism).

(iv)

When identified as an arrow
\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})_{/G}
, we have that the composition
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{U_{\leq n,\bullet}}\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))\xrightarrow{\textnormal{ev}_{1}}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
is naturally isomorphic to the constant map
\underline{\eta}:\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n}\rightarrow\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})_{/G}
.

Supposing that this
{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
-indexed family of maps
U_{\leq n,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
has been constructed, we will define
X^{\prime}_{\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
to be the map of simplicial sets induced by
(1)
(noting that
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}
is the colimit of the
\{(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\}
in the ordinary category of simplicial sets), which we identify as a natural transformation
\Delta^{1}\times(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
. Pulling-back along the map
\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}}\rightarrow\Delta^{1}\times(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}
induced by the canonical arrow
\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}}\rightarrow(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj}})^{\triangleright}
, which we further identify as a square
A:\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\textnormal{opp}},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
. Conditions (i)-(iv) now guarantee that there exists a
\tau
-hypercovering
X_{\bullet}
of
X\in\mathcal{C}
such that
h_{\bullet}^{\mathcal{C}}\circ X_{\bullet}\cong X^{\prime}_{\bullet}
and such that (up to isomorphism)
A
is a diagram in
\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta_{\textnormal{inj}}^{\textnormal{opp}},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
of our desired form

h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ X^{s}_{\bullet}
\underline{h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}}
\underline{F}
\underline{G}
\underline{\eta}
\underline{u}

The remainder of our proof will now go to constructing said
U_{\leq n,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
.

For
n=0
, we note that since
\eta:F\rightarrow G
is an effective epimorphism in
\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})
, it follows that the map
L(F\times_{G}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X})\rightarrow L(h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X})
is an effective epimorphism in
\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})
. Next, choose some small set
I
and an
I
-indexed coproduct of representables
\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}
along with an effective epimorphism
\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}\rightarrow F\times_{G}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
in
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
. Let
\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}\xrightarrow{e}S\xrightarrow{m}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
be the epi-mono factorization in
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
of
\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
. Since
L(\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}})\rightarrow L(h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X})
is an effective epimorphism in
\textnormal{Shv}_{\tau}(\mathcal{C})
,
m
is an
L
-equivalence, so the maps
U_{i}\rightarrow X
generate a
\tau
-covering sieve
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{/X}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{/X}
. In particular, we may choose a
\kappa
-small set
K
and a
\tau
-covering
\{W_{k}\rightarrow X\}_{k\in K}
such that, for each
k\in K
, we may choose
i_{k}\in I
and a map
W_{k}\rightarrow U_{i_{k}}
where the edge
W_{k}\rightarrow X
factors as
W_{k}\rightarrow U_{i_{k}}\rightarrow X
. This yields a morphism
\coprod_{k\in K}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{W_{k}}\rightarrow\coprod_{i\in I}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{U_{i}}
, which in turn yields a map
\coprod_{k\in K}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{W_{k}}\rightarrow F\times_{G}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
. Since
\tau
is finer than the
\kappa
-topology and
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
is a
\kappa
-sheaf, we have that
F\times_{G}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
is also a
\kappa
-sheaf: thus, the map
\coprod_{k\in K}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{W_{k}}\rightarrow F\times_{G}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
factors as
\coprod_{k\in K}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{W_{k}}\rightarrow h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\coprod_{k\in K}W_{k}}\rightarrow F\times_{G}h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
. The existence of the induced square

h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\coprod_{k\in K}W_{k}}
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
F
G
\underline{\eta}
u

in
\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
completes our construction of
U_{\leq 0,\bullet}
.

Next, let
n\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, and suppose that we have constructed each of the relevant maps
U_{\leq m,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq m})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
for all
m\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0,\leq n}
. Write
U^{0}_{\leq n,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
for the composition
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{U_{\leq n,\bullet}}\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))\xrightarrow{\textnormal{ev}_{0}}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
, and similarly define
U^{1}_{\leq n,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
. Next, right Kan extend
U_{\leq n,\bullet}
along
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright}
to yield an arrow
U^{n,R}_{\leq n+1,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))
. Write
U^{n,R,0}_{\leq n+1,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
for the composition
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright}\xrightarrow{U^{n,R}_{\leq n+1,\bullet}}\textnormal{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S}))\xrightarrow{\textnormal{ev}_{0}}\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
, and similarly define
U^{n,R,1}_{\leq n+1,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\textnormal{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\textnormal{opp}},\mathcal{S})
. Choosing some
X_{\leq n,\bullet}:(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
such that
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ X_{\leq n,\bullet}\cong U^{0}_{\leq n,\bullet}
, we see that
h^{\mathcal{C}}_{\bullet}\circ X^{n,R}_{\leq n+1,\bullet}\cong U^{n,R,0}_{\leq n+1,\bullet}
(where
X^{n,R}_{\leq n+1,\bullet}
is the right Kan extension of
X_{\leq n,\bullet}
along
(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n})^{\triangleright}\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{\textnormal{opp}}_{\textnormal{inj},\leq n+1})^{\triangleright}
), so we have the identification
